CYCLING ND MOTORING.

THE N.C.U. MOVES. I hear that the National Cyclists' Union is mov- ing in the matter of securing some reform so that cyclists wheeling their bicycles without a light at night shall not be held liable to fine. The Union is, I understand, taking the strictly constitutional line of acting through Parliament. and as it has been so largely instrumental in raising an immense !cycling corps for the Army, its representations should carry a great deal of weight. Meanwhile, ray advice to every reader is to support the Union by pressing•this matter home to the local Members of Parliament. HAVE A GEAR CASE FITTED.

If you have not a gear case on your machine I strongly advise you to have one fitted. without delay. And I advise this, fully realising how essential it is for all of us to exercise economy at the present time. A gear case isn't, of course, a necessity, but once one has experienced the advantages of such an accessory it really becomes very nearly a necessity. I would almost as soon omit mudguards as a gear case. But one word of advice. Don't, whatever you do, eo in for a very cheap one. , A poor one is worse than, none at all. A cycle chain is really an intricate piece of work, and unless it is looked after carefully, the ease of running is soon injuriously affected.

THE PRINCIPLES OF LUBRICATION. To ensure long life, freedom from trouble, smooth running, and efficient hill-climbing powers on a motor cycle, correct lubrication is essential. I don't think I am exaggerating when I say that it has more to do with satisfactory running than anything else. I shall probably raise a storm of protest when I say that there isn't one motor cyclist in ten who under- stands properly the principles of lubrication. The

SERGEANT G. HASLER.

Shot In The knee While Going Over

Parapet.

On Sunday morning Mrs. Hasler, 27, Napier- street, received a letter from Second-Lieutenant Edward H. Loman, South Lancashires, bearing date July 11th, stating that her husband had been wounded three days previously. . _ _

"My platoon," Wrote Lieut. Lo-man, "was advancing up a slope under heavy rifle fire from a hill on the opposite side of a valley. When on the top I turned to see my men go over, when a bullet passing through Sergt. Hasler's boot struck him behind the knee. It only passed through the leather of his boot and did not hit his foot. It dragged him over the top. We did all we could for him. He was very brave .while I dressed the wound. Sergt. Hasler has been my platoon sergeant since I have been with the battalion, and I always found him a good worker, doing his best for the men, and always my most willing helper. The platoon 'will miss him greatly. Neverthelets, we all hope that he mill soon return to his family safe and well, and for your sake we hope that the war will be over before he ever gets out here again." Sergt. Hasler was in the same battalion as Captain Chambers and Lieutenant Fergusson. Two days before he received his wound Mrs. Hasler got a letter from her husband couched in very affectionate terms. "I thank God," he wrote, "for His mercy towards me. I hope alio pray that the Lord will take care of me and protect me in the coming days." That was his last letter up to Monday, but on that mornity, she received another kindly letter, this time from the Rev. C. A. Sheppard, chaplain at a base hospital. Mr. Sheppard wrote: "Whilst visiting No. 3 Hospital yesterday (13th July) I found your husband amongst the patients. H.. has been wounded in the left knee. As soon as he can be moved he will be sent to a hospital in England. He is as comfortable as good nursing can make him, and you need Have no fear con- cerning him. He sends his love.to you and the

SERGEANT HASLER.

Wounded.

SERGT. FREDERICK C. NEWI3OLD.

Wounded.

PRIVATE J. 11. SMITH.

Wounded In The Leg And Progressing

Favourably'.

• Private 'John Herman Smith, of the South Lancashire Regiment, was wounded in the leg in the recent heavy fighting; and brought to a hospital in Sussex. From there he has written

===== 0002601_19160722_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATX J. 11, SMITH.

Wounded.

to his mother, who resides in Helsby-street, saying that he is progressing favourably. Private Smith, who is 19 years of age, is a native of Yorkshire, the family having been in Warrington about two years. He went to France on the 17th March this year, 'having joined the Army about four months pl-eviouslyi He was formerly employed at the Orford Tannery.

PRIVATE JOHN DIXON.

Struck While Rescuing A I,Vouncled

Comrade.

Private John Dixon, whose home is with his mother at 57, Plumpton-street, and who up. to a year ago was employed in the tube depart- ment at Messrs. Monks, flail and Co.'s works, when he joined the Lancashire Fusiliers, has been wounded in the head, and is now in the Northern General Hospital, Newcastle. He met With his injuries on July Ist, the first day of the advance, while bringing a wounded man into the protection of the trenches; and he has since heard that the rescued. comrade, .has, un- fortunately, died. Private Dixon attended Great Sankey School as a boy, and is now 23 years of age. He came through the Dardanelles campaign unhurt. His brother, Mr. George Dixon, Collins-street, who worked for Messrs. Crosfield, is in the local TerritoyialS; and his brother-in-law, Mr. Frederick Cleino, 5, Bissell7street, employed by the Mersey Lead Works, is also serving. Chief

PRIX-ATE CHARLES W. HEALEY. Saved From Explosive Bullet By Family Photograph Case. Another old Grammar School, boy in. the per- son 'of Private Charles William Healey, Man- chester Regiment, eldest son of Mr. Charles Healey, accountant, 6, King-street, has been wounded, and is at present recovering in hos- pital at BurnhVm, Somerset. His parents, who live at Orford, have just had a very cheery letter expatiating on the beautiful weather he is enjoying, and hoping to see those at home before being sent out again to France. Although his injuries were stated as "not severe," they were numerodS enough, and no one except the cheeriest and most courageous of young men could have made little of, them. In fact he Was perilously near death, and but for a steel case which he carried in his breast pocket—which _ .

field:^fa.-ncili'PllO-t-o-gi;pl-Private:Healey would have been killed by an explosive bullet. The case got, as he wrote, a little hummock at the bottom caused by the 4 bullet. In addition to the wound'an his chest, he was struck in the thigh and thumb, and received other smaller wounds, caused by shrapnel. Private Healey. was attachpd to the Intelligence Department, and went over with the fourth wave which swept to the attack on the Ist July. He was wounded after having been. in action something under two hours. His- battalion-, which 'cap.' tured a strong redoubt, was supported on the left by a Liverpool "Battalion in which there are many Warrington men. Although his wounds received some attention, he lay on the field for .about nine• hours before being brought' to the dressing station. After four days he was taken to Rouen, where he remained for a further similar period, and was then- brought to Eng- lantl.

, Mr. Walsh, 8, King-street, who has seen, the young soldier at Burnham, has written Mr. Healey a very heartening letter saying that he

is quite his old self in -spirit—".quiter the old school boy, not the war-worn veteran—although he has been through it and no mistake. I was delighted to find him looking so well." Private Healey, who is only in his 20th year,

joined the Army in September, 1914. and was nine months in Prance. He was educated at the People's College and- the- Grammar School,

Bowden, 1, Fothergill-street, has been wounded in the left arm and the 'head and brought to England. From hospital he . writes •home to tidy he is progressing favourably. • Private Bowden, who is 24 years of age, is one of three soldier-brothers. He was formerly , employed at Orford Tannery, which he left during the second week of the war in order to enlist. He has been at the Front eleven , months. He, ,was 'educated at the National School. The t.other two soldier-brothers are Pte. John Bowden, also of the Royal Warwicks, •svilo was badly wounded in the leg at the Dardanelles and is still in hospital; and 'Sapper Abraham, in training with the Royal Engineers. In addition, an adopted son, James Anderson, is akso serving in the Royal War-. wicks. .

MIGHT OF THE MITE.

Working Men To Kill Germane

With Silver Bullets.

SAVING ASSOCIATIONS TO

BE FORMED.

Although most of the working men rePre sentatives to a conference onvened by the War - Savings Committee in the Town Hall on- Tues' day evening, had no niandate, mainly being present to glean information, the ideas the/ expressed bespeak success' fofthe establishnient of War Savings Associtations in various works in the town.: These associations will be run by the men, 1v1)e will contribute h minimum of sixpence a weel" j and on 15s. 6d. having been subscribed between them, a War Savirigs Certificate ivill be pur- chased for 15s. 6d. In five years the certificate will be worth £l. The advantages oi these associations in the speedy acquisition of certificates is apparent, and we have no doubt that on the objects being understood working men will fall in with the idea with avidity. Apart from the personal aspect of saving money by means of these cer- tificates for their own advantage, there is, 'of course, the patriotic side. The Chancellor ef the Exchequer told us this week that the war is costing us over £6,000,000 a day, and, therefore all the money which can be spared by working men should be devoted to the purchasing of certificates, which are tantamount to silver bullets. Money is urgently needed for the carrying on of the war to a successful Con- clusion, and it is not overstating the position when one says that it is a case of "Your moneY or your life." In Manchester these associations have seivd the imagination—and money—of artisans, and as Mr. C. E. Patker, who presided at Tuesday's meeting, said,' in the first week they had raised over. £6,000, and last week their contributioDs totalled £27,000. • The Secretary of the local War Savings Conk mittee (Mr. Alex. Venables) explained to the meeting that associations in the various depart- ments of all works could be started with a membership of ten upwards, with a minimuni contribution of sixpence each per week, and then as certificates were purchased the 'members could ballot as to who should have them. MoneY spent upon things we could do without diverted labour from the manufacture of ,munitions goods for export trade, and there was also the important point that if the certificates were held until the end of the war the money which the owners could then spend would find emploY- 'tient for the-men retiring from the Army.' should not be forgotten that the cerfificateB could be cashed at ahy time without the owners having to wait five years.

===== 0002601_19160722_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
HARD TO SAVE, BUT POSSIBLE.

Mr. F. W. Baker, of Messrs. Rylands Brod., Ltd., agreed that everyone should do his utmost to save • and refrain from buying tinY- thing which could be detne without. "We can only do that by denying ourselves of Soul° things," he added. ,- "I do not say working men can deny themselves of a great deal, but it is possible that they can do something. It 15 taking people all they'can get' to live owing t° the exorbitant prices Leing charged, s(i that we must not hold out great hopes of obtaining an extravagant sum. There is, however, fl`l doubt that some can save, but the richer people ought to show a better example. While the working people of Gatwick were being asked t° save, the races were being held there, and n° fewer than 2,500 motor cars entered the course. These things do not incline one to save, arid wl think that -people in that position ought to show a better example." (Hear, hear.) Mr' Baker was of opinion, however, that many flier. in works could join an association, althougn there were plenty who could not afford to do so, as most of their earnings had to be spent on food. Mr. J. H. Smethurst said that at a works in Liverpool in which he was interested they had an ideal scheme, the firm having bought eel.' tificates for all the members of the associatipn, and as the men reached 15s. 64. a 'certificate was handed .over. By this means the men got in- terest in each case from the earliest possible date. The scheme was of great encouragement to the employes to saVe. "At my works in Warrington," he added, "I have put the mat' ter before the men and they have agreed t° form an association. I am, therefore, going t" buy twenty certificates for them on Saturdo3r, and they will receive them a they pay tile money."

ECONOMICS AFTER THE NX Xit.

Sir,—We have now had the Paris Conference 'l4l Economics, laying down some of the pre- tirainary rules for trade and commerce after the war. We have only to note the remarks, gooted from German papers, to realise what Alt,re the intentions of Germany at the close of cile war in regard to the world's trade and 'commerce. As I pointed out in my first lettef, Germany, for many years, has pursued a ,ay stem of exploitation in trade, which h•-. 41 .Leached The a 'Climax at the opening of the war. war was simply a device, by means of hich Germans intended to rivet the indus- trial yoke of their cguntry on the rest of the world. As they now see the probable failure of their military measures, they are straining awll their power to secure an economic victory, hich it is to the interests of the Allies to Vent. , A great deal has been said of late in regard .c creation of a Minister of Commerce, and ig interesting to review and compare the Iracle methods of Great Britain, Germany and . Ile 'United States. Taking the last first, we tnd the United States has what is called the reau of Foreign and Domestic Commerce vfitli its agents, in the character of Consuls, all _over the world, whose duty it is not to wait !jilt they are asked, but to investigate &lit. all ,„_"not may interest American commerce, and re- Pot to headquarters. "The Daily Commerce Port" is like an afternoon newspaper, going, Press in Washington before 10 in the morn- and circulating throughout the country. Ito G difference between the American and the e ~rnian is, in the matter of financial methods. :Mile the Americans supply the information, dleT do not incur any financial responsibility; the other hand, the übiquitous German agent 44tg always a fund at his disposal whereby he Llndermines the interest of the country to which a i 8 accredited, as was recently seen in the tr°e,edure of the attaches in the United States. has been repeately said, a man with a good ~„se• can easily dispose of it to the German -gent, who sees that the owner of the idea parts hit absolutely for Germans benefit. In -104_3land our Commercial Department is 'the i"4„l'd of Trade who, in good old English 3,7uion, never stirs until it is asked, and if ••,11. , . • Officious consul becomes a little active • t BCarcel— y conducive to his promotion. LOVE OF CONFLICT.

How It Should Be Usefully Applied.

The Rev. T. T James, preaching tha fifth of his sermons on The Church and the War" at the Wycliffe Congregational Church on Sunday, spoke on -" The Church, the War and the Young." '

The- relationship of the Church to the young,. he said, is the most important of all the ques- tions we have to face. If we look at things from the point of view of the Church, and ask our- selves what are the, prospect § of the Church in the future, what contribution she may be able to make to the best forces of our time, or how far she may- have a place in the progress of society, the,answer has to be sought ultimately in her relationship to the young life in her midst, her influence and hold upon it, and the possibilities of- that hold being maintained. If we look at things from the point of view of the State, and question ourselves as to its possible worth and stability and virtue, the answer has to be-sought in the attitude of those who are to make its manhood and womanhood in the future.

It is true on the whole to say that, at the moment, the Church has possession of the young. Vast numbers of older people are alienated, and in a lesser degreelyoung people in their teens become so, for the leakage in that respect is a grave and constant problem; but it remains true for the main part that the ‘Church has still within her borders the boys and girls and the younger members of the community. Their interests are served by definite Christian organisations, particularly the Sunday school, and by the direct teaching and training of Church life. It would be possible to spend time with profit in discussing this machinery for dealing with the young, but I want to go deeper than that, and give some attention to-night to the fundamental ideas and ideals for the pro- pagation of which such machinery exists.

===== 0002601_19160722_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
War Nurtured Generation.

- For this purpose in our day it is necessary to approach the problem of war in general. The present generation is being nursed and nur- tured in the atmosphere of war. Now there is la definite school of thought which regards war not as a passing evil, but as a permanent factor lin history, and one which the future will cer- tainly not remove. Its followers go further and declare that the pernlanence of war is a good thing, and that its removal would constitute a, disaster. The philosophy of Nietsche in Ger- many preclaims war to be a biological neces- sity, something which produces the moral qualities' of sacrifice, courage., endurance and 'strength. This is not • purely a German philosophy; it has wide following in other countries, and can notably. be discovered .in our own. It is held that the love of conflict and battle is inherent in human nature, that' some of the finest traits in human-character are its direct result, and that the cessation of war would consequently bring disastrous moral results. Instead of giving this argument a direct denial I. think we must appropriate such truth as it contains. The love of conflict and battle is inherent in human nature, and if you ask me how it is to be cast out, I shall say that it is not going to be cast out at all. But it is to be transfigured; it is to be put in a different setting. To say that human nature will always love conflict is not to say that its conflict is always to be set in the wild and barbaric terms of physical combat. The Christian philosophy maintains that the passion for conflict is to find expression on a higher and nobler plane, and Christianity presents itself as a struggle which has all the romance and adventure conceiv- able, and which demands all the qualities of sacrifice and endurance and courage, but finds its field of operation apart from the inhuman features of physical warfare. And if you ask what all this has to do with the relation of the' Church to the young, the answer is that it has everything to do with it, for the problem' is not that of the existence of organisations, but the ultimate ideas which organisations are to promulgate. The interpretation of Christianity must be made along these lines. The mission- ary effort must be presented as a world-wide adventure and romance demanding heroism and resource at least equal to that demanded by any military expedition to the far corners of the earth. The social problem at home must be presented as a crusade in which 'Christian effort will find an unexampled field for courage and for enterprise. The Entrenched Liquor Traffic.

Does anyone, for instance, suppose that the battle against the liquor traffic does not demand the highest moral qualities that any struggle can call forth? We are being told that the Germans are entrenched in France and Flanders so deeply that it is a most costly business to remove them—l know it and have seen it—but they are not as deeply entrenched as this traffic is entrenched in the vested inter- ests of our country, and it will not be as costly to.move them from their positions as it will be to conquer this. But that is only one problem. The foes of national righteousness are multi- tudinous enough. The time has arrived when Christianity must have something more to say upon the problem of poverty than the word that good people should be charitable to the poor, when the very foundation of society must be sifted and examined. There are a hundred causes demanding the finest and strongest qualities in their service if they are to win through to triumph. The City of God will only come by way of conflict. This, is the Christian knighthood to which the young are called, and Christianity must be presented along this line. We shall not eradicate the instinct for battle inherent in human nature, and we shall never by international conferences humanise warfare in the sense of physical strife, but we shall lift the whole conception to a higher plane, where in fairer and finer fields we shall discover a moral equivalent.

WYCLIFFE P.S.A.

The usual meeting was held on Sunday, being presided over by Mr. B. Farrar, who voiCed a vote of sympathy with Mr. and Mrs. W. Glover and Sergeant Glover, member of the P.S.A., who had lost his brother in action in France; also to Sergeant A. Spencer, who was in hospital with a breast wound received in action in France; also a congratulation to Private C. Norris, who had just got, married while on fur- lough. A message was received from Captain A. Hodson from France. Mr. Langton gave the address upon " Follow me.,.

The attendance was rather small, but there was a good meeting.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JULY 22, 1916.

• 4* • • • • 4* 4* 4* • 4* • • 4* • • • • • • 4* • • 4* • • • • • • • • • • 4* 4* 4* 4* 4* • 4* • • 4* • • +++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++++4. + ++ 4.+ •1:1" WOMAN'S WORLD •-o: 4* • + + + 4* • • • •

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

Women On The Land.

On a farm of two thousand acres in Glouces- tershire, women have now been employed for a year. On this farm there is a very large herd of shorthorns, and at least two hundred ,milking cows. The working day is divicled into three shifts, and the pay is threepence per hour. Fifteen women and girls last spring weeded one hundred acres of corn, cut the thistles on the grass lands, hoed and singled -forty acres of roots, and pulled and pitted the same acreage in the 'autumn. They also plough and harrow, and have become expert in both of these occu- pations. The farmer is now seeking a won to work the engine attached to the sterilising and refrigerating plant. So well have the women done the whole work of this farm that the proprietors have not only been able to keep up the supply of food, but sto increase it: Work begins early, but there is a good deal--of free time in the middle of the day, and three to fo:ir hours are available for rest and recreation. , Women As Analytical Chemists. _ - _

There are now over two hundred women on the register of the Pharmaceutical Society of Great Britain, and many more have qualified by a shorter course of training for the diploma of the Apothecaries' Society, which permits them to act as dispensers to doctors under a chemist or pharmacist. Many of these women are rendering valuable help to various Volun- tary Aid Detachment§ working in connection with the British Red Cross Society, Whilst others are doing good work in general hospitals. So far women have received but little enconragenieht to enter the higher branches of analytical chemistry, and the number preparing for the demand that will arise in the 'near future is comparatively small. Women scarcely realise how vast and far reaching the industry of chemical manufacture is, or that girls who have studied chemistry at school have just now a good chance of finding employment in the laboratories of factories, without having taken a degree. Ili some of the hospitals also, girls are replacing the boy 'clerks? and thus getting a foothold, which would have been denied them before the war. Smocks and Overalls-,

Many new ideas in women's dress- have been inspired by ,the war;. among others- the tea- frock, rest- gown, blouse-coat,, and' that modern marvel the coat-frock.. Ent even that practical garment is eclipsed by the• numerous,. attractive, and business-like overalls and smocks; with the short skirts that accompany them, provided for the, woman gardener;. labourer and m-unition worker. NOT has the, deSigp,er overlooked the needs of the -stay-at-home woman, who • in these days has to take upon herself the lion's- share of the work of the household. Most becoming are the otton frocks prepared' for indoor wear, during the morning 'working hours; most modest in price at all times, they' have been greatly rednced. 'during *he sales. Outdoor overalls and smocks are offered at ridieurodgy low prides in linen, crash, duck, drill, Casement cloth of a stout make, .and' cotton poplin. All are suitably decorated with stitcliery Of Various kinds. For the unprofessional woman there are the most delightful smocks and' suits In flowered cretonne. POckets are • a notable feature of all these garments. The White Blouse.

Notwithstanding the vogue for coloured blouses, this garment still holds its own in white, and thanks to the infinite variety afforded by collars, scarcely two alike, are shown this year. The loose sailor collar is for the moment in high favour, and is made in black or white satin, organdie, muslin, spotted chiffon, and numerous other materials. There are also many versions of the cape collar; one of which is closely pleated and turned over from a slow neck, whence it falls to the shoulders. It can- not he said that the basque4 blouse has met with much favour, perhaps because it suits only the tall and slim. A more becoming variety has a high waist, and a godet basque,.

which is only moderately full, therefore is less trying to the average figure. The lingerie blouse is particularly attractive tbis year, and many models offered in the sales are well worth the attention of bargain hunters. Very fine filet lace in-its natural colour, is used in the confection of the newest blouses of this type, and is most successful when combined with French lawn, voile, and very fine linen. In- sertion is usually preferred• to bordered filet, and this also forms the collar. Cauliflower.

There ,are many delicious ways of cooking ,this vegetable besides the familiar one of boil- ing it and Serving it with cream sauce. An excellent way is "an gratin." To prepare a cauliflower this way, select a large, fine one. Tear, off the leaves, pick the flowerets apait, cut off the roots, and wash it thoroughly in cold water. Then put it in a saucepan in abundance of cold water. Add a handful of salt,- and let the vegetable cook until tender. When it is cooked, drain it, put it in a baking- dish, and have ready a cream sauce, made by mixing a tablespoonful of flour with two table- spoonfuls of butter, stirring in two cups of milk with salt and pepper. Let the cream sauce cook ten minutes after making it. Add three table- spoonfuls of grated Parmesan cheese, and pour it at once over the cauliflower in the baking digh. Sprinkle over the cauliflower three more tablespoonfuls of Parmesan cheese, a table- spoonful of fresh breacicrumbs, and a few bits of butter. Let the cauliflower bake in a quick oven for about twenty-five minutes, when it should be a rich brown allover. A cream of cauliflower is a good soup. Mix a large table- spoonful of butter with two tablespoonfuls of flour. Add a teaspoonful of salt and a quart and a half of white stock. Put in half a small cauliflower which has been boiled. Let the soup cook thirty minutes. Then press the whole through a puree sieve and return it to the fire. Add a cup of boiling cream, and let the whole boil up for five minutes, stirring it all the time. ip,Serve it with squares of brown toast. Verminous School Children.

"The verminous child is still with us. Hun- dreds of girls with nitty heads are attending our .public schools and are constantly infecting clean children. The nifty girl does not always come from a poor home. It is no uncommon thing to find nitty hair in a child ,who other- wise is well cared for, and whose home is com- fortable and clean. It cannot be too strongly impressed upon parents and educational auth- orities that nits are the eggs of lice, and that each nit, if left alone, will become a louse in the course of a week. Nits require nourish- ment, and this they derive directly from the .blood. They destroy the hair, produce sores on the head, enlarged glands, and even abscesses and anaemia. Education authorities axe urged to deal stringently with such cases, and to make full use of the powers granted to them. The evil is deep-rooted, and more drastic measures will have to be adopted if our schools are to be made fit for the reception of clean children.":--Dr. Alexander G. Glass. The Mouth In Measles.

I contend that the first point of ipritry of the micro-organisms is the mouth, and in support of this submit that, in my, experience: (1) Every case of. measles with severe complications, has presented a mouth of exceeding foulness; 2) among my patients, since the adoption of routine cleansing of the mouth, no case brought under treatment within twenty-four hours of the appearance of the rash has had a fatal ter- mination. "The treatment adopted is simply to order the mother or nurse to wipe out the mouth carefully but gently every four hours with a piece of clean boiled rag or lint dipped hi borax or thymo-glycerine; attention is directed not only to the tongue and roof of the mouth, but especially to the inside of the cheeks and labial surface of the gums. I claim nothing new for this method. But from the number of instances where this simple piece of therapeutics has been omitted, it has seemed well to set down that which I have come to re- gard' as the most important detail in the treat- ment of measles, the disregard of which pro. d'uees results as unhappy as they are avoidable?' -Dr. Trist, "British Medical Journal."

to do their Dart, For every War Saving Cer- tificate bought for fifteen shillings and six- pence at the end of five years the country will pity to the holder one pound. This is a little more than compound interest at five per cent. Should the holder be obliged to cash these certificates before the end of the period he may do so at any time through any post office, but the interest which he would receive is not quite as much as if he allowed the Certificates to mature. There are distinct advantages in this method from an investor's point of view. He lays his money out at good interest. The pound he will receive at the end,of five years will probably buy as much is, say, twenty- seven shillings if spent during this time at in- flated prices. He, theraore, not only* saves in interest, but considerably increases the spending power of the money by .saving it until it will buy more. There is now no limit excluding those with lager income from in- vesting; all are invited to take part. The special advantage to the income-tax payer is that the interest on. these; Certificates is not subject lb income tax. An individual May invest anything up to the £5OO limit, that is £3S7 10s., which tecomes £5OO in five years. He may also invest this amount on behalf of his wife and each of his children. .. This is an important point; because the income tkx is sure to maintain a high leiel for five years and More. While, therefore, the investment is an attractive one to the small investor, it is still more so to those whose ordinary income is .sublect to the income tax deduction. This has been war ,savings week, during which the energies of those who are capable, of giving a lead have been concentrated upon the work of showing how money may be saved and usefully applied to the needs of the nation. At a meeting called in the Town Hall on Tues- day evening; at which there was a large attendance of working-men delegates, there was expressed a general' willingness to see that the advantages of saving by war certifi- cates were brought before the notice of the Wage earners. An association once formed in a works will be a means Of doing a great deal of good in more ways than one, while in the 'aggregate those .silver bullets which can alone finish the war will be foethcoming in sufficient number to dispose of ,the Germans finally and for good. We ought all to make a beginning, if we have not already done so, to save either individually or as a member of a War Savings Association.

THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, JULY 22, 1916.

ARE YOU PROVIDING SILVER BULLETS? Our men in France, bone of our bone and flesh of our flesh, are offering the costliest sacrifices for right and' for liberty. They are giving of their all without reservation, hold- ing back nothhig. Are we at home doing our full part? Do we fully realise that our heroes in France needein resources the backing of the whole nattion, of every unit that makes up the nation ? Have we realised as we ought that the victory of the Allies and al 4 the ideals involved ;in this victory depend upon which group of combatants can endure longest the financial strain? If everyone who can help will help there will be no question with which, side victory will finally rest. As we read of the heroic deeds of those at the Front many of us have wished that we could take a more definite part in this struggle. Here then is our opportunity. The boys fighting are doing their utmost, 'doing, in fact, more than their share. Shame upon us ,if because we cannot do the larger part we ,refuse to do the smaller. But our part is equally essential, first because every shilling which can 'be saled from ex- penditure on things not necessary means eas- ing the strain or the call upon labour. We cannot spend anything without involving the work of somebody, and if what we buy is not necessary we are doing an injustice to our ' country, an injury to our cause by absorbing labour which should be concentrated upon essentials, and an injustice to the poor by sending up prices. And, all important, if Our national resources become so depleted that we cannot continue to sustain ourselves and Allies in munitions and equipment, and as a result are obliged to make an unsatisfactory peace because of financial exhaustion, we shall be inviting a repetition of this terrible strife in a few years. ' The War Savings Certificates are the best method yet deviled for enabling those at home

===== 0002601_19160722_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
GOSSIP.

His, Worship the Mayor created a mild semi"- tion in the Town Hall last Tuesday, when he escorted a young lady through the building and finally took her into the Mayor's Parlour.

The bit' of. femininity was, however, in her nurse's arms, and is the latest -addition to the Mayoral household. She looked about the Parlour as if she had been in the plade before, and scanned with seeming interest.the pictures of civic magnates in the COuncil Chamber. She appeared -disappointed at not finding a familiar face and figure amongst the company.

She was subsequently taken for an airing in the Park, and not having made any complaint it is presumed that she was satisfied with the appearance and condition of the municipal pleasure grounds.

Nearly always at our Tribunals if an applicant happens to have more than the average number of children, some comment is made.

At the Warrington Rural District Tribunal on Wednesday, a man stated he had seven children.

"A fair number," commented one, and the Rev. A. M. Mitchell added, • " I have heard of a man who had thirty-two children."

"Not far fiom me there is one who has had twenty-two children," joined in the Chairman (Mr. George Mather).

The Tribunal looked surprised, and Mr. T. S. Steel voiced their feelings by saying "I didn't know they were so prolific in Padgate."

May I add that they are not Usually so? At last week's Board of Guardians it was agreed that owing to the few births taking place .in that neighbourhood the Registrar should only attend fortnightly instead of weekly. At that meeting nothing was said of the "Cabinet of Twenty-two." .

Is, it a mere coincidence that`the prohibition scheme should have been launched during the War Savings Week? It seems strange that we should make desperate efforts to pay for the war by saving sixpences when we are wasting nearly £290,000,000 a year upon drink, the manii- facture of which wastes other untold millions' worth of vitally necessary goods.

On Sunday night I saw a motor char-a-bane pass through Warrington. It was full of women, who were singing an dancing drunk. Yet some, or all of them, must haver had hus- bands aid brothers at the Front. They surely had, in the terrible words of the Rev. Percy Ineson, been _drinking men's blood. £200,000,000 a year would supply enough, shells to blow the Germans out of Belgium arid keep them out--- but we prefer to drink it! And to accept the consequences!

In fact, we are drinking, £20,000,000 worth more during war than in peace time—and/ 5,000,000 men away from the reach of our public. houses.

Insurance Chairman and Committees. At the annual meeting of the Warrington In- surance Committee on Thursday, the Mayor (Alderman Peacock) was unanimously ri:!. appointe,d chairman for the ensuing twelve months. The sub-committees were also re- appointed, the names of Dr. Manson and Dr. Murray being added -to those dealing with finance and eanatoritun benefit.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JULY 22, 1916.

DRINKING BLOOD.

Demand For Liquor Abolition

Till End Of The War.

PROHIBITION SUNDAY.

Last Sunday Warrington Churchgoers, with very few exceptions, had an opportunity' of signing th 6 form of petition, which it is hoped will assume gigantic dimensions, urging the Government to "prohibit the manufacture, im- port, export and common sale of intoxicating liquors during .the war and for six months afterwards." The papers were placed in the church porches, and the various clergymen in- vited the members of the congredation to append their signatures. In many cases numerous copies of leaflets dealing with the subjeet 3vere taken away for distribution, as well as forms of the petition for 'use amongst circles of friends with the object of obtaining as many signatures as possible. As in the case of the Rev. T. T. James, Wycliffe Congregational Church, Bewsey- street, several preachers directed the attention of their congregations to the war savings and thrift movement, and in addition appealed for their co-operation in the prohibition cam- paign. THE FEARFUL COST 'OF DRINK,

lireaching at the Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church, Rev. W. T. Derry said he had always regarded prohibition as an impracticable dream for this country, but its tremendous progress in Canada and especially in the United States of America had quite altered his opinion; also the example they had had in Russia. Cer- tainly the outside opinion with regard to the question of the trade had been affected by the war as it had never been affected before. They had found what a hindrance it had been in con- nection with raising an army of five million men, what a fearful cost it had been in time of their •financial strain, how it had decreased both their living capacity and food supplies. For [ material as wen, as moral reason's they needed I either prohibition or some very stringent measure either for the period of the war or the ,period immediately succeeding it. The forma- tion of a War Savings Association in connection with their church was necessary partly because the country absolutely needed the -money now, and partly 'because the people would need it when the war is over.

lie mentioi*d the war savings certificates bringing in five per fflent. compound interest, or the equivalent of seven per cent. if they were income tax payers, and told his hearers that they had already one such association in the church, started by Mr. Upson's Bible Class.

===== 0002601_19160722_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
ABUSE OF COMPROMISE.

"Drink prohibition immediate" was the dominant note of a vigorous sermon by the Rev. H. Ogden, at the Baptist Church, Golborne-street. His text was Judges i., 28, "And it came to pass when Israel was strong that they put the Cananites to tribute, and did not utterly drive them out." The text, he said, was ail evidence of th'e Misuse rather than the abuse of compro- mise. Israel was instructed to exterminate the Cananites, but the leader S discovered that it Paid ,better to tax them than to exterminate them. There may be money without work and income without sweat either of body or mind, and that is evident while the human race is self- indulgent. Before the war 'there was a need of prohibition. Investments increased and in- vestors increased—bishops, vicars, and evtm ministers added to their emoluments from brewery_ investments. The .ilamage that was done, physically and Morally, was beyond com- pute, and English life even before the war needed prohibition. How much more necessary is it during the time of , war merely' upon the grounds of economy? Having referred to the huge sum of money spent for drink and the vast amount of food- stuffs used in its manufacture, the preacher said what concerned him most was that all forms of drunkenness at this time were not merely regrettable, but they were, as their Chief Con- stable said, criminal, and the man who sold to such an excess and the man who drank to such an excess were equally guilty. During the times of the reduction of hours there had been abund- ant evidence of less drunkenness in the streets; the Police Court had not been thronged on a Monday morning with drunks from the week- end, and all social workers had rejoiced in that. It was suggested from that there is more private drinking in the homes, a suggestion that has not been sustained; but it is true that there is some drinking in the homes, and knowing that the children are in closer contact with it, the more clamant has become the call for ‘immediate pro- hibition of the drink that up to now has so curtailed the nation in its efforts.

===== 0002601_19160722_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
DRINKING THE BLOOD OF MEN.

The Rev. Percy meson, preaching at Bold- street Wesleyan Church on "Love and War," tpok for his text I. Chronicles ii. 19: "Shall I ,drink the blood of these men that have put their lives in jeopardy." Likening our own Army to the brave men who risked their lives to bring David water from the Well of Beth- lehem, he asked were we going to regard the heroisin ,and sacrifice of our soldiers and sailors L-securing our food and winning our'freedom— as sometlting sacred? We could drink the blood of these men in several ways, particu- larly tby excessive expenditure and excessive pleasure-seeking and iirdulgence. Referring to the expenditure on intoxicating liquors, he said he approached the question not so much from the standpoint of the Tem- perance advocate as from the Patriotic. In the first tWenty months of the war our drink bill was 300 millions—a sum which would have paid for the cost of the war for two months and so reduced our huge burden of debt, which the men who are sacrificing ' now. will have to help to pay off when they return. •Again we have drunk their blbod. This sum, moreover, would have provided our men with 300 new aeroplanes every day, of the war, or it would, have built one hundred and fifty new, super-Dreadnoughts, or ' have given our men ,80 million cartridges a day extra supply* But these things which might have hastened the end of the war are not -ours and England's sons are yet in peril, because we are drinking the blood of those who are jeopardising their lives :for us. Can we continue to imperil the lives of those who love us by letting the traffic continue? "I want you," said Mr. meson, "to sign the petition to the Government asking that the manufacture, import, export and com- mon sale of intoxicating liquors be prohibited during the war and for six months after. . . We are quite untrue to those who are fighting for us if we do not think and strive to remedy the state of affairs that has brought this,awful catastrophe upon us."

WOMEN LIBERALS.

Sewing Party_Givespp Its Holiday For

Our Solders:

A 'communication having been addressed to Mrs. Osborne, hon. secretary of the Women's Liberal Association Sewing Party, it has been decided to cancel the holiday which had been arranged for August. Every week since the beginning of the war scores of these ladies have assembled in the John Crosfield Memorial Hall and engaged in knitting comforts for our soldiers, and it was thought that they might have a rest during August. But a letter hav- ing .been received from 'the Secretary of the Voluntary Organisation Scheme pointing out the urgency with whiclr comforts were; needed, they have decided to press forward instead of taking a well-earned rest. At this time sit is particularly gratifying that Colonel Crosfield has come forward and offered the use of the .Tower House Gardens, in Lodge-la,ne, to these ladies on Monday after- noons. On Thursday of last week about fifty of the members went to Southport and had a day's enjoyment in excellent weather. Soon after tea Mrs. Osborne had a great surprise, when Mrs. Richardson rose, and, on behalf of the Sewing Class, handed her two lovely cushion covers as a token of appreciation of her ser- vices as their secretary. When they met on Monday this week Mrs. Osborne wvi again surprised, for the members supplemen d their presentittion with some beautiful table glasses.' At the same meeting they presented a fully- equipped bed to the Thelwall Heys Auxiliary Hospital and a box of croquet to the Oaklands Hospital, Mom.

===== 0002601_19160722_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
WHEN, WAR WORK ENDS.

Unemployment Insurance Ex- tended To Additional Trades.

LOCAL TRADES AFFECTED.

The proof of a summary of the scheme under which additional classes of workmen are to be insured under the Insurance Act, from Sep- tember 4th, ha; just been issued. The schenie is two-fold, and comprises the ordinary com- pulsory arrangement involving contributions by employer and employed; and the encourage- ment of voluntary insurance against • unem- ployment by State grants to associations in all trades and occupations which pay out-of-work benefits.

All persons over sixteen in. the following trades are to be insured against unem'ploy- ment :—The manufacture of ammunition, fire- works, and explosives; chemicals, including oils, lubricants, Soap, candles, paints, colours, and varnish; metals and repair of metal goods; rubber and goods made therefrom; leather and leather goods; bricks, cement and artificial stone, and other artificial building materitds; saw milling, including machine, woodwork and the manufacture of wooden cases. - It will be seen that the trades mentioned include the leading Warrington .industries. Many of them are engaged largely upon .ivai work, and • this extension is evidently meant to cover the unemployment that may ensue when the output of war material is suddenly stopped.

===== 0002601_19160722_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
WORK ON

And Help To keep The Hun

On The Run.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

• Mayor's Parlour, Town Hall, Warrivgton, 21st July, 1916. Sir,—l have received a letter from the. Mi- nister of Munitions stating how important it is that at, the present time an undiminished out-. put of munitions should be obtained. For this purpose it appears necessary that all holidays, not merely those of workmen directly engaged on munitions, should be postponed. have written in• reply, Saying that I feel confident that an appeal to the patriotism of the workers of Warrington and the public generally to postpone the, August holidays is one which will .be cordially agreed to and that Warrington will do its best to co-operate in the great offensive now in progress. ' As the Prime Minister has so ably stated, "No great or more direct service can be ren- dered by the workers of this country to the Forces in the field than to give the latter the encouragement and 'out enemies the discour- agement of the certaln knowledge that the present intensity of bombardment will, if necessary, be continued indefinitely." .

I am, Sir, , Your obedient ,servant, PETER PEACOCK.

HOUSING PROBLEM.

Works' Sanitary Conditions Not Fit For Pigs.

At a ,meeting of the Warrington Insurance Committee on Thursday, the Mayor presidi4, a report by Mr. P. Delaney and the Clerk (Mr. Alfred Tilling), as representatives to the National Association cif Insurance Committees, which met in London, was read. On the ques- tion of the housing conditions in,towns ittstated that the attention of the L2cal Government Berard had been drawn to the fact of the ,greatip wastage /of human life and of public 'money, and the defeat of the operation of the Insurance Act by overcrowding and insanitary conditions in many parts of the country. There was an urgent .necessity for, more effective legislation to remove these conditions so seriously operating against the fruits of sanatorium benefit. This had been emphasised by the Board. Some Insurance Committees asked the Execu- tive Council to suggest to the Commissioners that owing to the decrease in amount available for sanatorium benefit owing to the transfer of a large number of insured persons from civil to military life, the per, capitam sum of a penny allowed. for the purposes of research be credited to cf,st of sanatorium benefit. The suggestion was not agreed to as it was con- sidered excellent work. had been done by the Medical Research Committee. ''

The Mayor moved acceptance of report, and that thanks be given to their representatives for same.

- Mr. E. Rigby, seconding, agreed, particularly with the part dealing with housing and sani- tary arrangements. Not only was improve- ment wanted in the way of housing, but also in some ~of the works in the borough, ,which were not fit for pigs, much less human beings.

Municipal Houses?

Alderman Bennett said the housiat problem required their most serious consideration, and it would require real 'statesmanship to solve it. If they got rid of insanitary building how was accommodation to he provided. The obvious suggestion was , that the municipality should build. -He was not opposed' to that, but as a Council they must consider if they could build upon economic conditions—build suitable houses with suitable air spaO, and at the same time get such a return that would justify them in spending money on an undertaking of this character. With reference to the provision of funds for the benefit of research, he thoroughly agreed with the decision. Prevention was bet- ter than cure all the world over, and he thought this money well spent. The report was adopted, and Mr. Delaney and Mr. Tilling were again appointed as repre- sentatives of the committee on the National Association. ,

V.A.D. WORK.

The Great Need For Special

Service Nurses.

The inspection of the Voluntary Aid Detach- ments of the Dareshury DiviAon of the British Red Cross Society in the Cairo-street Schools last Saturday was the occasion of a very plea- sant gathering. The detachments represented were :

Cheshire 22, Commandant Miss M. Broad- bent.

Cheshire 48, Commandant Miss Monks. Cheshire 128, Commandant Miss Johnston, Cheshire 150, Commandant Miss JOynson.

This was the first Ome the fouf detachments had ,met together. Tea, arranged by the quartermasters of the detachments; was served. This was followed by an inspection by Iklro. Jarmay, vice-president of the Northwich Division, the cadets being drawn up by their officera. Mrs. Jarmay was very pleased with their neat appearance in the orthodox Red Cross uniform. After thanking the Vice- President of Northwich, Miss Sylvia Broddbent, vice-president of the Daresbury Division, spoke of the work of the Voluntary Aid 'Detachments, and gave, the latest regulations of the society. She made an appeal for offers of help 'in hospital, saying that those who could give part-time only' might enable those who could give full-tilne to be spared in some cases from local hospitals for special service in military hospitals at home or `abroad. ' The need for special service nurses was very great, since the great influx of wounded men into the country; and Mrs, Furse (the commandant-in-chief) had appealed to Cheshire to continue the supply of nurses where it .could be done without depleting the lotal hospitals of the necessary staff. . Miss .Bamfield (matron of the Lord Derby Hospital) gave an inspiring 'address on . the ideals of hospital work and the spirit in which nursing should be undertaken, saying that in tilis time of national stress the best that any of us could give was not good enough, that the best should be made better, and that it was a privilege to live now' when there was so much opportunity to be Useful: A most hearty vote of thanks was passed fo Miss Bamfield for her goodness in coming to address the Red Cross nurses when she had I had such a atm/lupus fortnight receiving num- erous ,convoys from the Front.

"OUR DAY."

Preparation For Red Cross And

St. John Ambulance Event.

Judging by the enthusiasm and .whole- heartedness of the, ladies' committee, "Our Day," to 'be held in WarringtOn and 'district on the 19th October., 'will prove a lute success. It is not necessary to urge the claims of the British Red Cross Society .and the St.' John Ambplance Brigade—their work speaks, for itself, 'especially' at the present time, when. we have such a gigantic Army in the field with its attendant list of casualties. The Mayor (Alderman Peter .Peacock) pre- sided on Tuesday at a public meeting in the Town Hall, called to make preliminary arrange- ments. He understood that cafe chantant had been suggested, and he had arranged that the• Parr Hall should be at their disposal.. The band of the &mill Lancashire Regiment had .been promised, while Mr. War- burton had' kindly offered the Royal Court Theatre for a 'matinee.. The S. John Ambu- lance Brigade might be asked to organise, a whist drive, and , private organisations would also make 'efforts the' same as last year. Miss SYlvia Broadbent, vice-president of the paresbury Division; hoped that Mrs. Fox, the township leader, and -her committee would organise ,the , effort in Warrington, and that othe* " leaders would join in helping the work. Mrs. Forshaw, who was unable to be present, had intimated that she would be quite prepared to help.. A strong working committee was formed, with Mr. F. Joynson, 'honorary .treasurer for the Warrington fund, to act in a similar capacity.

SOLDIERS' TEA PARTIES.

Refreshments For Nothing On CAPTAIN AND MRS. J. MONKS'S Sundays At Bold Street. DIAMOND WEDDING. Coming into the town on Sunday afternoons and finding all cafe doors barred against them, wounded soldiers were extremely gratified to learn last week that by popping into the Soldiers' Recreation Room in ,Bold-street Wes- leyan Church they, could enjoy .an excellent tea in comfort and without expense to themselves. On weekdays soldiers visiting the school obtain food and drink at cost price, and, therefore, Sunday's free menu is even a greater boon to them. Nearly 300 men in khaki and blue availed themselves of the privilege, and were not re- served in their expression of thanks, for the irksomeness of returning to Winwick is intensi- fied by Absence of refreshment when in town. Convalescing soldiers require frequent nourish- ment, but when they are out they have not always the wherewithal to purchase it, and, even if they had, they ate denied the opportunity on Sundays." The Sunday school children having been dis- missed hist week, some of khe workhs re- arranged the large room with tea-tables, which were interspersed between the seven billiard tables—the temptations of the greeh being partly removed by white clpth coverings—while others hastened with the Cutting and spreading of bread, the brewing of tea, aid the assembling of luxurious comestibles—such as cake, pre- served fruit and. lettuce—into appetising forma- tion.

Captain Joseph Monks, a Warrington gentle- man, celebrated, his diamond wedding on the 19th inst., an event which aroused considerable interest in Liverpool shipping civles. He was born in Warrington, in 1837, and lived .in his native town for over' sixty yehrs. On retiring to private life, at the age of 70 years, he went to reside at Egremont. More than twenty years ago he founded the firrp. of Joseph Monks and Co. Ltd., steamship owners and agents, having their offices at 30', Brung- wick-street, Liverpool—out of which also sprung J. H. Monks, Preston, Ltd. The two firms are the owners of. thirty-two steamers, six of which are on His Majesty's service. Along with ',the 'late Mr. John Crosfield, Captain Monks founded the Mission and Sunday schOols at Bank Quay and laboured there for 'a great number of years. He has designed and built a large number of steam Vessels and is followed in the business by. his three sons, the ..eldest of whom, Mr. Joseph Monks, resides in Stockton Heath. Captain Monks's life .mate was a .Miss Porter, who was born in 'the year 1838 and belonged to a well-known ' Warrington family'.

It had been announced to the frequenters of the .rooms during the week that they would. be .welcome for two hours from'four o'clock. Very soon after that time Scouting parties were-peep- ing in wondering whether all was ready, and soon the place was agog with rattling tea cups, cheerful faces and afternoon tea talk. Occasion- ally a soldier went to the Piano or organ and, of his own initiative, played hymns, which was right and meetiso to do. That the soldiers, whether wounded or not, will appreciate these free teas on Sunday after- noons almost goes vithout saying. Although many staying at the Lord Derby 'Hospital have found friends who insist upon their regular acceptance of invitations to their homes, others are not so fortunate, and, therefore, the privi- leges of this , recreation room are of great pleasure and usefulness—friends indeed when in need. It may be mentioned that on Sunday the wife of a blue-coated soldier arrived from some distant town on a visit to her husband, and, the cafes being closed, both of them sought tea and were given it with every grace at the recreation room. And all for nothing. A feature which greatly pleases the visitors is the absence of fuss and any hint of denom- inationalism'. No matter what sect their identification discs proclaim, they are all wel- come. Their particular religion is of no more importance on these occasions than the fame of their regiments or the parting of their hair. At many places soldiers are given penny cakes for a halfpenny and tracts for nothing. Here on Sundays everything is for nothing="-and there are no tracts. The institution" is undenOmina- tjonal. The cot of this innovation is considerable, of course, and we trust it will receive the support which will permit' of its being continued until the dawn of peace breaks, and the shadows of war flee away. Mr. A. Lumb is the secretary and Mr. W,illiam Isaac treasurer. •

3s. RISE.

Hundreds Of AVarringtori Labourers To Participate.

Mr. Seabury, secretary of the • Warrington branch of the National Union of Gas Workers, informs us that by an award of the Committee of Production many hundreds of unskilled, and semi-skilled labourers in the borough and district will benefit to the extent of 3s. weekly. This is the second increase st 4 3s. weekly which the labourers have received since the declaration of war, the wage now totalling 28s. The new advance "is to be 'regarded as war wages, and recognised as due to and depending on the existence of abnormal conditions pre- vailing in 'consequence of the war."

Important News for Students. At a meeting of the Education Committee 01 Monday, it was decided that the school certit cate examination of the Northern Universities Joint Matriculation Board be recognised as the examination for the award of senior scholar- ships and bursaries, and as the qualifying examination for admission to training colleges; and that half the amount of the examination fe,e be returned to a candidate who reaches n ", minimum" standard on the first occksion, and the whole of the examination fee to a candi- date—who' reaches a "satisfactory " or higher standard on the first or subsequent occasion of taking the examination, and that the portions Of the Secondary School prospectus relating thereto be. amended accordingly.

Local Composer. • We would call our musical readers' attention to two pianoforte compositions composed by our townsman, Mr. Frank Booth, organist of Walton Church, and published by Bayley Ferguson, music publishers. "La Debutante" is the title given to the first piece, and it is in waltz forra and in the key of A. It is a well-written, bright and piquant composition, and will "not present much difficulty to the young pianoforte student. "La Coquette" is a la minuet style, in key of G, and contains some rather inter- esting passages, especially in the middle move- ment of ,the piece. The taedulation to this section is rather abrupt. Both compositions, however, are worthy the attention of budding piapists and should prove useful and ,instruc- tive pieces. "Absent Friends" ,is the . title given to 3 little composition for pianoforte by J. Warwick Moore and published by the Orpheus Music 'Publishing Company. It is in the style of .8' meditation, and, while net very, pretentious, 18 of .a very, tuneful nature and is worth while purchasing.

Hit While on Rescue Work.

Private Frank Dutton—also a Warrington man, who lived in Wellfield-street, and his com- rade in the same Field Ambulance—writing to Mrs. Car/isle on July 14th, sail: "I can't find words to express my sympathy; in fact, to talk of sympathy and condolence is useless, for they are of no avail. We can only be glad that he died swiftly and suffered no pain. Poor Jim! While out between the trenches to rescue the wounded lying there, he was hit by a stray bullet, and in a few minutes all was over. He left no message—never even spoke--r-it was ,so swift. I have been requested by all his chums to send to you the deepest respect, to which I myself add my hcaitfelt sympathy." •

SECOND A.M. JAMES SIMPSON.

Died of Wounds.

exploded near him and he was rendered un- conscious for a few minutes. It was found that he had been wounded in the left thigh and also that his left arm had been broken, and his left foot and leg both lacerated. "I visited him in hospital, and, knowing the serious condition he was in, was very surprised to find him so bright and seemingly undisturbed about his wounds. Ile neither. 'complained of his hard luck nor wounds, but took everything lighflY and made us believe that there was every hope of his recciveiy." Simpson's leg'had to be amputated and a few days later he ,passed away. In a letter received by the parents from his Lieutenant it is stated: "He was one of the oldest and most trustworthy operators in the squadron and his loss will not only be ex- tremely felt 'among his comrades, but by myself."

The Chaplain has written a letter testifying to the fine character of the deceased: "He was a true soldier, brave, manly and clean, a genuine Christian, and, I need hardly add, a devoted son." •

Second A.M. Simpson was only eighteen years of age, and went to France last September, having left the Post-Office a few months be- fore, he then being a learner in the telegraph department here he was very much respected and it has been decided to frame an enlaiged photograph to place in the room in which he and his colleagues worked together. Mr. Simpson was a Sunday school teacher at Silver-street, and attended Bewsey-road Wes- leyan Church,

PRIVATE L. KENDRICK.

One Who Always Volunteered For Dangerous Work.

Private Leonard Kendrick, of the *Dual Lancashire Regiment and a native of York- shire, has been killed in :action. News to this effect has reached Mrs. Morgan, 190, Longford- street, with whom Private Kendrick resided while in Warrington. Captain J. A. Pilson wrote as follows, under date July 14th :—"lt is with deepest regret I haVto inform you of the death of Private Kendrick, who was killed in action on July Bth and died a soldier's death. He was a brave fellow and his death has proved a great sorrow to us all. Please allow ,me to convey to, you my deepest sym- pathy, and that of the whole company, in your sad bereavement." Under date July 12th, Private J. J. Balmer, a comrade, wrote :—"I aseure you it upset me very much to hear of the death of Private Kendrick, which happened just as I was about to have a chat with him. I cannot speak too highly of his courage in the field. He was alwaye amongst the volunteers for any dan- gerous task and was herd in high esteem by the other boys of the platoon. Though it is hard to lose .a good and jolly 'chum,' it is consoling to think that he passed away whilst plodding the path of duty." Private Kendrick was a single man, 23 years of age, and became acquainted with Mrs. Morgan whilst .stationed at Orford Barracks, and lived at her house before leaving for an- other training centre in England.

REPORTED KILLED.

PRIVATE M. ROGERSON. Rejected Five Times And Then

Accepted.

Mr. ana Mrs. M. Rogeirson, of 146, Longford- street, have been unofficially informed of the death in action of their son, Private Mathew Rogerson. The news was sent by Company- Quartermaster-Sergeant T. E. Newton, of Earle- stieeti to whose company Private Rogerson belonged. Confirmation of the news, howeder, is not yet forthcoming. Private Rogerson, who was 22 years of age, enlisted in April last year, after having been rejected five times. He had-been at the Front since January, where he had acted as officer's servant. He was formerly in the employ of Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co., Ltd. He was educated at St. Alban's School, and at- tended St. Benedict's Church. His brother, Michael, was wounded whilst on active service, and has been discharged from the Farces.

J..McLeed Campbell, senior chaplain "You will have received before you get this letter a postcard from your son, Alfred, report- ing that he is safe. I fear up till then you will have had a very anxious time, if the news that he was missing had reached you from the War Office. I am thankful that this terrible time of anxiety has now been ended. Your boy was wounded in the attack on July Ist, in the legs. He was unable to move more than a short dis- tance at a time, and he escaped the notice,...of the search parties who were trying to bring in the wounded. So he has gone :through a terrible 'ordeal of days and nights in the open, between the opposing lines, and it was only yesterday morning (July 12th) early that he reached this ambulance. lam glad to he able to tell you that his wounds have kept wonderfully clean, and the doctors hope that all will .now 'go well— tliOugh, of course, 'they are Serious wounds; one leg for the time being has.lost tts power. He was marvelously calm, and content, and had kept himself splendidly steady all 'through as trying an experience as could befall any man. You may, indeed be proud that a boy of yours,. aged '2O, should have shown such an heroic spirit. 1 promised to write to you—but to let his own card get home 'first. He will •be able. to write himself when he gets, to hospital. He was in- quiring especially • about his bombing officer, Second-Lieutenant Clark, of the R.B. MORGAN.

One Of Four Soldier-Sons Wounded In The Hand.

One of the four soldier sons of Mr. and Mrs. T. lorgai4 33,. LiverpOOl-road, has been wounded in action. He is Private ' Peter Morton, of the -Inniskilling Fusiliers, and he has been slightly injured in the right hand. PRIVATE F. RATHBONE.

AVounded In Left Hand, But

Progressing Favourably.

News has reached Sirs. Rathbone, of Hume- street, to the 'effect that her husband,, Private F. Rathbone, of the Royal Welsh Fusiliers, has been wounded in the left hand_ In a: letter home, _Private Rathbone says: "Our lads got the better of the Germans; and we got to the place we were told to take. When we got close to the Germans they asked. us for mercy, but they, did not get much, I can assure you." Private Rathhone, who is a former employe of Messrs. Why-man's Foundry in Dallam-lane, enlisted the month war brae out, and has been at the Front twelvemonths. He was home on leave a few Months ago. He Was educated at St. Ann's, School:

XTE H. BALSHAW.

Accidentally Injured And Taken To Hospital.

. _ Private H. Balshaw;, who joined a Cycle Corps soon after the outbreak of" war, but was sub- sequently transferred to another regirdent, has been accidentally injured with a pair of wire .cutters 'whilst- 'in the trenches,. and has been removed to hosPital. He has written to his parents,. who reside at 4,8, Marsh House-lane, saying that he is 'progressing faveurably. "We were in the trenches for two days," he added, "and it was two days in hell:: _Private Balshaw, who is 26 years .of age; enlisted immediately after the outbreak of war,

along with two bothers and a —arother-in-law. He Was educated ,at the , National School, Church-street, and was employed in the barbed wire department Of the Whitecross C.o.'s works. An all-round sportsman, he was the best shot in the Whitecross shooting team; of which he was a member. He was also an efficient billiard player. Of the three other relations who enlisted with him, only one is now serving. It is his brother Jack who is "somewhere in Franca" with the Ammunition Column of the The other brother, Fred, served in the R.F.A. for nine 'months, and was then discharged tt medically unfit. He has tried several times since, but has not been successful in re-enlisting. The brother-in-law, Mr. Ernest Willett, was also discharged after serving' with the local Territorials. He had been a member of, the battalion for about sixteen years previals to the war.

Howley, and a scholar at St. Mary's Butter- , market-street,. was,. before the war, ,engaged in. the service of the Zillah .Shipping Co. His brother, Seaman John Cullen, ,who lives with him at 90, Cartwright-street, formerly worked for Messrs. Monks, Hall and Co., and is now on. an Australian transport. His brother-in-law, Mr. George Smith, Moore , Post Office, is serv- ing with the Cheshire Regiment in. Mesopotamia. , ii . .' DISTRICT MEWS 11

LIYMIVI

wounded soldiers from, Brookfield gave one of their comrades a hearty send-off.

I am very pleased to notice that some thoughtful person has placed . a seat at the bottom of the new Lever-road for the benefit of our wounded heroes. .

Off-Licence.—The off-licence at Market Cross, Lymra, was, at Altiincham on Monday, trans- ferred from Isaac Henry Evans, deceased, to Frederick Ernest Evans.

Departure of Wounded Soldiers from Brook- field.--Stirring scenes were witnessed through- out Thursday morning, when seven members of the " Lymm Garrison" departed from the Brookfield Hospital, fully recovered from theii wounds, to partake of the usual leave granted prior to re-joining their regiments. As each man went away his comrades accompanied him to the station and gave hini a ,royal send-oil. Talking to an "Examiner" representative, one of the gallant soldiers stated :—" We shall never forget the kind treatment we have re- ceived while at Lymm, both from the mem- bers of the hospital staff and the public in general. Every Where we have been made wel- come, and I can assure you we take away many pleasant memories of , the old ,village.:" The names of the men who left on Thursday morning are :—Private Thompson, K. 0.5.8.; Private Sidebottom, K. 0.5.8.• Private Hen- shall, Manchester Regiment; :Private Paynter,, Royal Fusiliers; Private Kavanagh ("Paddy"), Royal Inniskillings; Private -Hynds (" Little Tich "), Cameron Scottish Rifles; and Sapper ,Goodwin (" Cecil"), Royal Engineers.

Grammar School Pupils and Parents Make Merry.—Pleasant scenes were witnessed at the Grammar School on Wednesday afternoon, when the parents and pupils met in the spacious grounds to take part in a garden party. One of the principal attractions was a cricket match arranged between lathers and sons, and created considerable amusement. The boys lia.l matters all their own, way and won easily. The masterly way in which they handled the, bat and ball spoke volumes for the splendid train- ing they have received at the hands of their sports master. An exhibition of work executed during the past year by the scholars, including a large number of shirts, wearing apparel, splints, &c., which are to be handed over to the British Red Cross Association. It is interesting to know that the Material for these articles was provided from the money value of the prizes which the children annually receive. This year they sacrificed their prizes for this worthy cause, and were granted certificates in their place. They have also collected throughout the school for the same end upwards of £26. The girls came first with contributions amounting to £lB. During the afternoon tea was daintily served on the lawn.

Baptist Ann ive rs a ry.—Anniversary services of the Baptist Sunday 'School, Higher-lane, were held on Sunday, the Rev. Edward Parker, of Leicester, being the preacher. Special anni- versary hymns were rendered by the scholars during the day, and solos, afternoon atnd even- ing, by Miss Eva ,Tanner (Warrington) and Miss Winstanley (Warrington). Miss H. Brundrett and Mr. Atkinson (Warrington) were the organists. In the afternoon a young people's service was held, when a number of visitors from neighbouring Sunday schools were present. The chair was taken by Mr. Edwili Brown (Lymm), who, in a very apt address, appealed to all branches of the Sunday school to c'o-operate in promoting the success of the "Nursery of the Church." Air. Parker's address was specially directed to the boys and girls present and was greatly enjoyed. The subject was "The Call of Matthew." After the afternoon service tea was held in the schoolroom for friends from a distance, Mr. Lovell (the pastor), and Mrs. Lovell acting as host and hostess. There were good congrega- tions all day and the - collections will be de- voted to The work of the Sunday school.

LOCAL FACTS AND FANCIES.

By CRITIC.

Through the departure of Mr. Farrar Lymm has boat one of its "shining lights."

But not owing to the liglating restrictions.

A. testimonial was presented to him on Saturday night as a memento of the happy hours he spent in the old village.

Will someone please make artin,gements so that we can publicly recognise our soldier heroes?

Remember that Lymm is the proud possessor clf a D.O M. and a D.S.O.

A local soldier writing from Franc t says that around them the Germans are as thick as peas •

Shell 'em, boys; shell 'ern!

Fun and frolic reigned (supreme at the garden party held at the Grammar School on Wednesday. -

One of the printipa/ attractions was a cricket match between the fathers and the boys.

,Although the fathers put up a good show, youth told the tale, and the boys scored an easy victory,-

The pick of the fathers' team was Councillor Critchley, who, in the second innings, which was not completed, made,33 runs.

The strength of his batting was shown in the fact that he lost three euccessive balls in the adjoining hayfields.

Considerable amusement was created when, ort. atterhpting.to make a run, he collided with his partner, who went to earth with terrific force.

The "swearing in" ceremony of the Volun- teers still continues. Several new members took the oath. at "Lymm during the past week.

A demonstration will take place at Heaton Park to-day. The Lymm and Oughtrington Platoons will be there.

The first "swearing in" ceremony of the Oughtrington Platoon was conducted by Mr. Samuel Davies on Wednesday night.

FREE CHURCH FEDERATION.

To Help Anglicans In National Mission.

Although the number of visitors to Lymm on Saturday afternoon in connection with the summer meeting of the Manchester, Salford and District Free Church Federation was extremely small, the gathering was most representative. As the Rev. John Heath, secretary, remarked, there were delegates present from Swinton, Fal- lowfield, Sale, South, South-East, \ East and North Manchester, Eccles and Salford, and their President; the Mayor of Altrincham, -had bepn able to come. It was hoped that that would be the forerunner of many meetings of fellow- ship in that delightful part of Cheshire. Leaving Manchester at 2 p.m., the party travelled to Heatley Station, where they .alighted and were met by Mr. J. D. B. Smith, secretary of Lymm Free Church Council.: In the course of a delightful stroll to Lymm a visit was paid to Warburton Parish Church, where the Vicar (Rev. G. Egerton-Warburton), who greeted them very cordially, took them thiough the ancient building. At 4.30 tea Was served at the Primi- tive Methodist Church, Lymm. Mr. G. F. Armitage, Mayor of Altrincham, was in the chair at a short business meeting which followed. The Secretary read several letters from the committee and others who are making preparation in Manchester for the Church of England National Mission of Repentance and Hope, thanking the Free Church Federtitipn in cordial terms for their resolution of sympathy with the objects of the mission. The Secretary Said he was recommending- all Councils in their area to do the same to show members of the Anglican Church that they were at one with them in the reat work they were attempting.

It was stated in connection with the national campaign for prohibition that the movement was being taken lily energetically by the Churches, and that they would further the project of get- ting a"gigantic Petition signed. The speakers at the public meeting which fol- lowed were the President, the Rev. W. H. Mor- ton, Manchester, and the Rev. Professor Huin- phries, tutor, Primitive Methodist Cmllgge, Man- chester.

The President urged that they shotild keep their churches and church life going on the highest standard and prove to their soldiers that they valued their churches and all they • stood for. They should also keep the men's wives in the churches and their children in the schools. Referring, to the drink question, he said that notwithstanding all the restrictions statistics showed that the consumption was greater last year than in previous years—proving that drink vtas taken to the homes. No effofts must be spared to rescue the home, and the drink habit must be curbed until/it is no longer a danger. PrOfessor Humphries was of opinion that in the church things would have to be somewhat different to what they had been when the boys &lie home. He did not believe their denomina- tional distinctions had beenT the reason why j
