THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JULY 22, 1916.

PETTY SESSIONS.

Before Vice-Chancellor Stewart-Smith (chair- man), Messrs. I% 3.. Ridgway; S. W. Gould, S. Davies, T. Lockyer and 11, Holden. Illegal Treating.

William Dunning, farm' labourer, was charged by Constable Bolsover with having, on the 26th June, advanced money to another labourer named Thomas Cullen, for the pur- pose of obtaining _intoxicating liquor at War- burton. Cullen wu charged with consuming drink on licensed •premises, purchased by the money so procured. Both pleaded not guilty. Constable Bolsover stated that when cycling past the; Saracen's Head; Warburton, he saw Dunning slipping some money into Cullen's hand. Witness cycled back and saw Cullen inside the public-house, drinking a pint of beer. He challenged Cullen, who stated that he got the money for tile beer from the man outside. He asked Dunning about the matter and he said he did notithow it was an offence there.

Cullen: You asked me what the man gave me, and I said 31d. You never asked who paid for the beer.

The men are both farm labourers from New- ton-le-Willows, and Dunning told the Court that he had only given Cullen his own money. The bailiff on the farm had given him a shil- ling to divide between them. The Chairman asked if that were so why he did not give Cullen 6d. instead of 31d. Dunning was understood to say that the rest had been spent. Cullen said he had been 42 years with the same employer and had never been in any trouble. Each defendant was fined ss. Separation Order Granted.

Mrs. Emma Wadsworth, Sandy-lane, Lymm, prosecuted her husband, Peter Wadsworth, for alleged desertion, and asked the Court for an order of judicial separation. She stated that she had been married to defendant, for fifteen years and had lived for that time at Lymm. There were ,no children. Nine weeks ago her husband went away and he had been "a single man" ever since. He did not give her any money. Coming to par- ticulars, she described a visit she paid to the Farmers' Arms to get her husband, and hav- ing got him they had a "few sharp words." He struck her and called her a certain name— which she handed up to the Bench on a slip of paper—and, fearing further violence, she locked him out. She afterwards unlocked the door, but he had not returned since. Her husband was a salt worker and earned 30s. a week, and was stopping with his sister. In reply to her husband, complainant denied having struck him first, but admitted that she struck him after. She was not drunk, but "just had a drop." "How many times," asked defendant, "have you taken up knives to me?" "How many times," replied Mrs. Wads- worth, "have you given me black eyes?" Defendant was proceeding to describe vari- ous attacks made upon him by his wife with knives, forks, plates, and ,cups and saucers, when Mrs. Wadsworth interrupted most vehemently, causing the Chairman to exclaim, "Keep quiet, please; this is no, place for your squabbles." Defendant Edratted that: he hied not given his wife any money for the past eight week. "I am in danger of my lifd,"- he -.went on; "there is no comfort at home—she is always drinking. She May be steady for a month or two, but other times she is not able to walk home. My money is 265. 6d., and sevenpence goes off that for the club. _ _ _

Mrs. Wads*orth told the Bench that she worked with the farmers when it was fine.

The magistrates granted a separation order and directed defendant to pay his wife Bs. 6d. a week. He was also ordered to. pay 7s. 6d. Court costs.

===== 0002601_19160722_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
FARRAR TESTIMONIAL.

Lads' Club Leader, Scoutmaster And Volunteer Sergeant.

A pleasing ceremony took place in the Assembly Room, Conservative Club, on Satur- day night, when Mr. James. Gill Lister Farrar, .yrho is leaving Lyrnm for Buxton, was pre- sented with a cheque and album, as a little recognition of the valuable services that he has rendered Lymni during the past 10 years. Mr. Farrar came to Lyrnm 10 years ago as a newly- married man, and since that time his one object in life has been to help others, and so popular in his good work did he become that at the present day he is termed the "idol of the youth of Lymm."

===== 0002601_19160722_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
His Work With The Lads' Club.

A few years ago Mr. Harry Pearson, a well- known local man, who by sheer determination and ability rose from the bottom of the ladder to an important position in the commercial world,_spoke to Mr. Farrar of the need of a club for the young men of Lymm.: A meeting was called and took place in the Newfields Mission Room, and it was there decided that a club should be formed' and held ift the Drill Hall, and that Mr. Farrar 'should be the leader. The good work at mice proceeded, and the lads were taught. gymnastics, boxing, fencing, wrestling,, shooting, sports of every description, and Supplied with the best litera- ture obtainable. In short, everything was done to make the youths of Lymm good citizens and a credit to the village. Since the club com- menced over 200 Lymm lads have passed through the hands of Mr. Farrar, 60 or more have served their country during the present trouble, and six have made the supreme sactifice. That in itself speaks volumes for the good work done by Mr. Farrar.

===== 0002601_19160722_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
Organiser of Scouts.

At the commencement of war Mr. Farrar de- cided that the youths of Lymm who were net of military age should be given a chance to assist their country, and he at once set to work and formed a troop of Boy Scouts. Re himself became Scoutmaster. If anyone has doubts as to the value. of Boy Scouts ask '"Tommy," and he will tell you what they have done for him both at home and "out there."

===== 0002601_19160722_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
Popular Volunteer Sergeant.

Another of Mr. Farrar's favourite pastimes was in connection with the local platoon of Volunteers, in which he held the important rank of Section Sergeant. Nothing more need be said about his valuable assistance in this branch than that said by Mr. G. IL Williamson, his Platoon Commander, speaking at the testimonial gathering on Saturday night. "No one will miss Mr.. Farrar more than 17 Mr. Williamson said, "for no one attended drills more regularly. If ever I wanted any special work done and placed it in Mr. Farrar's hands, I could always rely on it being done satisfactorily." His Connection With The Church.

Mr. Farrar also took a deep interest in the Church. Shortly after arriving in Lymm he was elected sidesman, then churchwarden, and sidesman once again, and for some considerable time he held the office of church secretary. Nothing was too much trouble for him, and .ts the Rector recently said, "All these good quali- ties are due to his sterling character." The testimonial gathering was presided over by Mr. W. N. Molesworth, who was supported by the Rev. G. B. Thurston and Messrs. Alf. Watkin, R. Oliver, T. F. W. Dixon, G. R. Williamson and H. Pearson.

The presentation was made by Mr. Alf. Watkin who, in a few words, thanked Mr. Farrar on behalf of the people of Lymm for the valuable services he had rendered during the past 10 years. Mrs. Farrar also came in for a fair share of praise. Replying, Mr. Farrar said he was very much affected, and explained that had it not been for the splendid way in which Mrs. Farrar had assisted him by not tying ' him to her apron strings he would not have been able to do what he had done. _ .

"For he's a jolly good fellow" was heartily sung, and the National Anthem left everyone happy in the knowledge that the work of a good citizen had been‘properly recognised.

===== 0002601_19160722_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
MILITARY TRIBUNAL.

Mr. T. J. Ridgway, ohairman, presided at last Friday's sitting of the Lymm Military Tribunal. Mr. Fred Evans was the military representa- tive.

===== 0002601_19160722_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
Railway Clerk Gets Exemption.

A railway. clerk, who has almost reached the allotted span of life so far as military service at present is concerned, sought exemption on domestic grounds—principally on account of his wife's indifferent state of health. Applicant also mentioned that being employed on the rail- way he should be considered as being engaged in work of national importance. He wanted about two months of being 41 years old.

The railway company did not appeal. Dr. Burrows having given evidence, filo Tribunal granted exemption on condition that applicant joined the local Volunteer Force.

===== 0002601_19160722_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
General Hawker's Appeal.

Temporary exemption was granted in the case of a general hawker, ironmongery, and hard- fare dealer, who appealed on domestic grounds. He has a wife and four children, the youngest being only four months' old. He is forty years of age and the business, which he has 'worked up himself, requires him to visit other districts with his cart five days each week. He also has a little shop. Goldbeater's Application.

A married man with two children, and who is in his 39th year, asked to be exempted on business grounds. He is the works manager of a local firm'of gold-beaters, and he pointed out that the firm, as well as other English firms, was making a determined attempt to capture the trade hitherto enjoyed by Germans.. Six emPloyes of the firm were now serving their King and country, and they had been replaced by six boys, who were under his teaching and supervision. There were also fourteen girls in the shop and three men, gold-beaters. Through a misunderstanding his employers had not appealed on his behalf, but he alleged that if he were taken the work would become dis- organised and the other hands would be thrown out of employment. , Temporary exemption was granted until 14th September. Short Exemption For Dry Goods Trader.

An application for total exemption was filed by a dealer in job stuff, who is a married man, aged 39 years, and who has resided in Lymm about 12 years. He explained that he had built up his connection in six different towns after strenuous work for a number of years. He worked the markets in Preston and other towns, where. he was obliged to rent storage accommodation for his goods. His expenses in travelling, &c., were heavy, and he was entirely dependent on the business. From constant ex- posure in the open-air he had contracted chronic rheumatism,

In reply to the military representative, he said he did not attest because his business was his whole life's work. Temporary exemption until August 14th was granted. Applicant, who was evidently much surprised at the decision, expressed his intention to appeal.

Builders' Employes.

Messrs. Thomason and Sons, builders and con- tractors, appealed on behalf of two of their men, one a bricksetter and the other a painter. The bricksetter had been examined by the medical board at Chester and was passed for home service. He was given conditional exemp- tion until Ist November. In the cas9 of the painter, exemption was granted until/ August 14th.,

"A Word of Than ks."—Writing from Heaton Park to our Lymm representative, Corporal Linley says: "Will you kindly thank the people of Lymm on behalf of myself and comrades for the kind way in which they entertained us last Sunday afternoon; also for the many kind offers of kindness that we were unable to accept. We were sorry to have to come on so sad a mission, .and our deepest sympathy lies with his loved ones."

===== 0002601_19160722_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====

STOCKTON HEATH.

Presentation to Newly-married Sunday School Teacher.—To put into tangible form their good wishes regarding her recent marriage, St. Thomas' Sunday School teachers presented Mrs. A. J. Thorp with a drawing-room asbestos stove on Wednesday evening in the Parish Room. In the absence of the Vicar on holiday, the Rev. E. W, Clarke presided over the meet- ing, and in making the presentation to Mrs. Thorp, eulogised her work both as a teacher and as a worker in the parish. Mr: Wightman, a Sunday school teacher, spoke in similar terms, as also did Miss Barnes. Mr. A. J. Thorp, replying to the presentation on behalf of his wife, expressed his wife's and his own high appreciation ,of their tgood wishes for their future welfare and happiness. He said that it was not so much the intrinsic value of the gift as the kindly thought which accompanied it they valued so much. Mrs. Thorp 'also ex- pressed her grateful appreciation of the gift, and said that it had been a labour of love to work amongst them.

GREAT SANKEY.

L ITE LIEUTE\ I.NT JONES.

Warrington Vicar And The Vicar's

Fallen Son.

A service in memory of the late Lieutenant J. H. Ryle Jones, of the South Lancashire Regiment (son of the Rev. J. R. Jaes, vicar of Sankey) who fell in action early in the great advance, was held at St. Mary's Church last Sunday morning. There was a large congre- gation, and the service throughout was of a most impressive character. The Rev.; B. ',Percival, Vicar of St. Peter's, Warrington, preached from Revelation vii., 14, "These are they which came out of great tribulation." On Ascension - Day, said the reverend gentleman, when 600 children attended his own church, he asked all who had fathers, brothers or uncles serying in the Forces to hold up their hands. He was astounded at the forest of hands that shot up. There was hardly a , child who had not some near relative serving. ' Lieutenant Jones had a call in Canada, and he could not resist it. He walked no less than 70 miles to get to the nearest recruiting station. On coming to Europe he was called to a posi- tion of danger, and still he obeyed. Last of all he was called into God's Presence. He (the speaker) had had opportunities of speaking with men who come from the trenches, and he had learnt that even men who never prayed at all under ordinary conditions prayed there. "We feel," he said, "that our friend, who had been brought up as a Christian lad, knew where to look for help and guidance,, and that lie was really called into the presence of God. As the Bishop of London recently said : If we could see our lads who have been called into the presence of God on the playing fields of heaven, I we could not have the heart to wish them back here again.' There owas a great nspiration in the death of those who had sacrificed all for what had been called 'the nailed Hand against the mailed fist: " The organist (miss M. Standish) played tae Dead March in " Saul " at the conclusion. The hymns included " Through all the chaning scenes of life," "'or all the saints who from their labours rest," "0 God, our help in Ages past," and Through Pie ;light gt LIMA and dorrow.'t

THE LATE SERGEANT BROOKES.

His Comrades Attend Memorial Service.

A memorial service to the late Sergeant Brookes was, held in the Primitive Methodist Church on Sunday evening, and was conducted by the ,Rev. R. W. Ferguson, the Primitive Methodist minister. A striking feature in the proceedings was the attendance of fifteen of the late -Sergeant's comrades from the Con- valescent Camp at Heaton Park, Manchester, under the Command of Sergeants Bostock, Dutton and Wadelin, and ten local comrades, who had been discharged for various reasons and are at present at home. The party assembled at The Cross and marched in a , body to the church, where special pews had, been reserved for them. Other notable persons I present were all the late Sergeant's relatives,l Quartermaster-Sergeant T. W. Field, D.C.M., and Mr. R. B. R. Hartley, who represented his brother, Captain G. W. C. Hartley, the late Sergeants company commander_ .t The hymns chosen were all the late Sergeant's favourites and included "Yes, God is good," "City not made with hands," "Rock of ages," "Jesus, lover of my soul,", " Ged moves in a mysterious way," and "Lord, while for all mankind we pray." Addressing the large congregation, the preacher said : "WhoP will show us any good?" Henry Brookes was mobilised in A uguft, 1914, and was drafted out to France in Febrdary, 1915, where he distinguished him- self, serVin,g on the battlefield, rendering first- aid to his comrades. 'How proud we all were when we heard that he had won a high Russian order. He was carrying out his duty on July 11th of this year, 'after getting back from a furlough, when a trench *mortar ex- ploded, injuring a party of men, including our friend, Henry Brookes. Although 'he fully realised his danger he was quite calm and peacefully composed to the end. When the end came he just turned on his side and went to sleep. Two years ago last April I officiated at his wedding in this church, and less than twelve months ago I baptised his baby at Heald Green, Cheadle. On June 3rd of this year I was travelling from Broadheath to Lymm. when I happened to come across Henry in the train, and what struck ,me most was the way in which he abstained from speaking about himself. Not one word did he say about his own heroism, or how he won the coveted honour. I asked him how he liked the Army life and he replied: "It is not an ideal life, but we have to make the best of it."

The reverend gentlemaii apologised for the unavoidable absence of Mr. and Mrs. Henry Champ, his late employers, owing to Mrs. Champ's father passing away, that morning. A special prayer was also rendered for the benefit of the deceased Sergeant's wife and child.

===== 0002601_19160722_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
Captain Hartley's Testimony.

Writing home to his people, who had been inquiring as to the fate of Lance-Sergeant Brookes, Captain Ward Hartley. Brookes' com- pany commander. says: "Yes, I fear it is quite true about poor 'Brookes. I was awfully sorry to hear of his death, for he has been quite one of my very best men since I came out here, always willing and raostireliable."

===== 0002601_19160722_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE THOMAS DRINKWATER.

Wounded In Face And Arm.

Mrs. Drinkwater, Rush Green, has this week received a communication to the effect that her son, Private 'Thomas Drinkwater, South Lancashire Regiment, has been wounded in the face and ann. Writing from a hospital camp, "somewhere in France," the gallant soldier says :—" Will you please tell my many friends that I am doing well? My eyes are very weak yet. and I cannot write too many letters. Will you pleaSe let me have some news and a paper -as soon as you' can, for I am longing to know how; the dear old village is' getting along? We are very close to a 'big town and in a very big camp. We could not wish for anything bPtter and we get plenty to eat. I am quite all right 'and the only drawback is that there are none 'of my pals from the regiment here but we make pals with one' another. I shall want several things when I get well, such as a razor, &c.,' 'for all my kit got 'buried by the same shell that hit me. Have you heard any news from George?--[another soldier brother]. I do hope that he Is safe." Private Thomas Drinkwater is a well-known local man, who enlisted in September, 1914, joining the Cheshire Regiment. After a time ,he Was transferred to his brother's regiment, the South Lancashires. He was drafted out. to France about November, 1915. He is 20 years of age and was educated at the Lymm School and worshipped at the Lymm Parish Church. ,Prior to the war lie was employed as a -brick-. :#44ter for Mr. Levi Thomasson, contractor, New-road.

BURTON WOOD.

JACKETLESS POLICEMAN

Detects Gamblers And Gets The "Pool."

When Thomas Mullin, of 7, Fairclough-stree•;, Burtonwood, and Thomas Wilde, of 44, Jackson - street, were charged at the Warrington County Petty Sessions on Wednesday Nfith playing pitch and toss, P.C. Clarke stated that he Qaw the defendants gaming, and, being at 'a disadvantage through wearing a uniforM, took off his jacket and approached them in that way. He got close to them and scooped up the eightpence which was on the ground. ' Mullin was fined 7s. 6d. and Wilde, who gave a wrong address, was fined 10s.

NEIGHBOURLY SOLICITUDE.

As an instance of the way that local people have of helping one another, it would be hard to find anything better than this. Read over every word of what a neighbour tells you here, and judge whether- anything could be more helpful to a man or woman down with kidney trouble. On September 14th, 1905, Mrs. E. Howard, of 3, O'Leary-street, Orford-lane, near the Beamout Schools. Warri,pgton, said:—" It was a cold that brought on mr kidney trouble. Terrible pains started in my back. After stooping it was a hard struggle to get up again. When I got warm in bed I could hardly turn, and I often cried with the pain. It was almost unbearable. " I heard that Doan's Backache Kidney Pills were a good remedy for such troubles, so I got some and gave them a fair trial. These pill 3 made my back feel easier, and after taking them regularly for a short time I was cured. I aro quite freed from all signs of kidney trouble, and shall always speak well of Doan's Pills. (Signed) "E. Howard." On February 19th, 1916—over ten years later Mrs. Howard said All these years I have kept well, and free from symptoms of kidneY complaint. I still take an occasional dose of. Doan's Pills, for I find they keep me strong and well." Doan's Backache Kidney Pills relieve the kidneys and, bladder_ like ordinary medicine; relieve the bowels. They drive out the uric Lida poison which is the great cause of backache, rheumatism, and lumbago, and they release the accumulated water in cases of dropsy and., retention. They are perfectly safe to use in al' circumstances, and a certificate of purity accom- panies every box. Of all dealers, or 2s. 9d. a box, from FosterMcClellan , Wells-street, Oxford-street, London, W. Co.,Don't ask for backache or kidnel pills—ask distinctly for Doan's Backache ICidaei Pills, the same as Mrs. Howard had.

• Wanted Uniformity. Colonel Henry Watterson, who collects odd, curious, and amusing advertisements of a Per; sonal nature, tells of this one, which appeal*" in a Kansas paper:— "WANTED—To trade a horse for a mule or a mule for a horse; it makes no difference which. The idea is this—l have got a mule and a horse, and want two of a kind."

Would know why. • Quaker Meek had a very good horse and very poor one. When seen riding the second turned out that his better half had taken the good one. "What," said a bachelor, a lin. comes it that you let your wife ride the betle' horse?" His reply was: "Friend, when thee beest married thee'lt know."

PENKETH.

Wesley Guild Bowlers:—Last Saturday a bowling main in connection with the P3nit9th Wesley Guild took place at Heath-road Bowling Green. A most enjoyable time was spent, tea being provided by Miss Booth. The prizes were distributed by Miss D. Baker to the following ladies and gentlemen :--.Mrs. Stone (six start), Mrs. A. Bowyer (six start;, Miss ,Booth (six start), Mr. T. Booth (scra-,z,h,, Mr. F. Davies (scratch) At the close Mr. F. Davies pro- posed a vote of thanks•to Miss Booth for the use of the green and providing tea.

School Children's Effort—On Thursday afternoon the children of the Penketh Council School held a bazaar in the school hall, for the purpose of raising money for comforts f-sr our wounded sailors and soldiers. Mrs. Murray introduced Mrs. Griffiths, who, in a very stirring and ihteresting speech, appealed to trie patriotism of all present, and reminded each one of the deep debt we all owe to the: men who have been doing things for us. She then de- clared the bazaar open. This is the third and biggest affair the children have, arranged abso- lutely on their own initiative, Mr. D. Griffiths. the school master, quite emphatically repudiat- ing any credit for himself or the teachers. The stalls were arranged and served by the' boys and girls of the older classes, and would not, have been a discredit to an experienced bazaar pro- moter. Miss Sybil Cook proposed and Master ISyd.,Jones seconded a vote of thanks to all who had contributed to the bazaar either money or goods.

===== 0002601_19160722_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
PENKETH SCHOOL GENERAL MEETING.

Penketh School general Meeting was held on Thursday, a large company of parents and friends thoroughly appreciating both the class- room work and the entertainment. The walla were decorated as usnal with the work of pupils, and there was an exhibition of woodwork by the boys and needlework by the girls. A Satisfactory Year.,

The reports of the School Committee and the Head Master spoke of a successful and pros- perous year. Mr. Brown dwelt on the fact that the school was full to overflowing in spite of the great diffi- culty of running a school at the present time. The science master, Mr., T. IL BrunYee, had been called up for military service, and his place had been taken by Mr_ W. M. Bailey. Mr. R. W. Sheldon had been passed for home service only, and so was able to continue teach- ing. The Head Master was therefore able to congratulate himself on maintaining his staff at its full strength. There was small cause to wonder at the adverse balance shown by the accounts. The cost of living had increased 59 per cent., and yet the average cost per child was only up 29 per cent., pointing to a policy of rigid economy. The , school had been inspected by Charles E. Stansfield, of the Friends' Central Education Committee, who, in a most satisfac- tory report, dwelt especially on the excellea morale of the school.

===== 0002601_19160722_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
Working for Honour.

In the evening the certificates were given out by Mr. John Ransome, of Warrington, who re- marked on the absence of prizes both for class work and for sports. He pointed out how much finer a. spirit it showed to work or play for the honour, of winning than for any prize, however valuable. The same applied to after life—any- thing worth doing was worth doing well. Life was a striving after a succession of ideals, and each thing well done for its own sake brought us nearer our ideal.

A miscellaneous programme was rendered by the pupils. Piano duets were given by Hannah Jolleys and Dora Whitworth, Alice Kelsall and Connie Greaty; piano solos by Winifred Brown, Dora Whitworth and Hannah Jolleys; recita- tions by Marjorie Knowles and Phyllis Taylor.; part-songs by the Girls' Choir; French recita- tion by Peggy Robinson, Dodds and Chatterthn; and the funeral scene from "Julius Ca3sar" by Jessop, Jessie Griffiths, Isobel Seddon, Hart-

CHAPTER VIII. - - A MIND IN TUE PAST. Already was Mrs. Lamar Stanleigh beginninir:to hap the: whirlwind. The ideas she had for years encouraged in the budding mind of her son were. hearing forbidding fruit. While she alone held the "gret of his attack upon Dr. Lee, knowledge of his mattered little, but this woman calling herself im-rs- Hagar Harding knew as much as she—more, LP fact. The intruder—the widow could reztrd her ail nothing else—held photographic records suffi- cient to place the hangman's noose around Blair's If he had brought this upon himself he must neiti her to find a way out of the impasse. But where was Blair? Neither in the house nor the rounds had Blair responded to her calls, and, arful of the future, the woman returned to faze Ragar singlehanded. It was with a fierce resolve.* in a sudden revulsion of desperation and destair. that Ws. Stanleigh returned to the living room to tell her strange visitor to do her worst, let the consequences be what they .might. For herself, the austere Mrs. Stanleigh resolved to strive no more for the sake of her dissolute and desperate son. " I will make no bargain with this woman, even 1? save Blair from the gallows," was her grim 'nought "Neither she nor the strange girl ,who Was Dr. Lee's ward, and whom she now mothers at Stanleigh Hall, shall have social countenance from me." Thus concluded the austere Virginian aristocrat. • As she neared the room she heard hurrying feet, and thought that Blair had returned. At the threshold of the room she stood aghast! The room „was empty. What did it mean, and why these Many signs of a struggle? From near the fireplace came muffled groans and a low, moan of pain. Pressing the spring of the hiding-place, the wall swung back, and there in the semi-darkness lay the form of Hagar. Into the room she dragged the manacled woman, and turn- ing to the table for the keys of the handcuffs saw that the incriminating photograbhs had gone! "It , must have been Blair," whispered she, walking to release Hagar. Fully expecting a savage outburst, the widow was , surprised at the change in her visitor's expression. Defiance had softened to a pathetic, cowed wistful- 'lee& Her face seemed younger, her eyes moist and Pleading. She turned to Mrs. Stanleigh with out- ' stretched arms and murmured, "Give me my child, the child you took from me and sold." Then tlair's mother saw that the blow her son had dealt the strange visitor had deprived her of all realise- tidn of the present. Mrs. Stanleigh had no suspicion of the import of agar's words, but she realised the crazed woman was no menace to either herself or her son, and she led her' downstairs and ,to the door, where she set her on her way to Stanleigh Hall. Home she walked to Stanleigh Hall, where, in the gathering twilight, Esther awaited her. The dull eyes of li„agar gleamed with recognition of the portals of ule house, but the face of Esther was the face of a l!tranger to her. Alarmed, the girl led her up the "road steps, and then Hagar pressed towards the library. Here a fierce, wild change came over her. °De. ning.the door of the room she said, "See, he is in here!. They have him in there, the child they stole and sold from me! See the diamond that blazes on his little breast! Take off the diamond! Tljere is a curse on it for all our race! Take off "Lc fine raiment: give him back his raga! He is lair son!" •,,As it had been with 'Blair Stanleigh's mother, -r%ather attached no importance to the wild plead- of Hagar. Now, too. even the crazed mind of .ctagar seemed to realise the library was empty and unoccupied, for she gave a wild cry and fell sobbing the breast of Esther. The blow Blair Stanleigh ad dealt Hagar had turned her mind back to the Pad. • • ()V,ll below Esther heard a disturbance, and 'UrrYing found Luke Lovell ordering Quabba icrwlLY- Without a frietd in the world, now that "Qagar was strangely ill, Esther temerdbered 'the .7eat service the hunchback organ-grinder had ran- Arthur Stanleigh. There was something so I Yavl , ° tric in the hunchbacra 'rgsTActs,,l riri h,A kvii, Slade Esther take his nd- after sternly dismissing the scowling Lovell, 'and beg the humble hunchback to stay. "Do not 'cave us," she whispered, "something tells me that 3'°l3. will be a friend, and we have no friends now, Bave• Perhaps, our gipsy peoplc...l The eighth Earl of Stanleigh had passed to his fathers, and Marmaduke Smythe had come to merica in search of the heir. Twenty years be- -I°re he had come to Stanleigh Hall to verify the 'irth of the heir, and now he had come in search

of Arthur, who. his father and uncle being dead, was Earl of Stanleigh and owner of the estates in Warwickshire. This he explained to Esther, whom he found talking to a man with a monkey. • It was Esther's painful duty to tell the strange caller of the accusations azainst Arthur Stanleigh, of his flight and disappearance.

"My word 1" exclaimed the man from London. "If the American Earl is a criminal I must notify the next-of-kin. Blair Stanleigh, whom I remember as a vicious little beggar who bit me. I hope he has outgrown his wicked propensities as a child." Accordingly the man of law drove over to see Mrs. 'Lamar Stanleigh, aid there he received confirma- tion of his belief that the youni man had not outgrown the wopensities of his childhood. Blair's mirth& informed the' startled lawyer that her 'son, now next in line for the proud Stanleigh Earldom, had fled, no one knew whither, and she hoped never to see his face again. All she would say in reply to the barrister's nervous pleadings-,for he dreaded a long searcli. for the heirs of Stanleigh in bar- barous' America--vms that Blair-might be found at the home of Mrs. Burton 'Randolph, her cousin, in Richmond. For that place the lawyer took the first train from'Fairfax. , Hoping that association Ivith old friends would restore Hagar's mind, Esther the same night took her to the gipsy encampment. But it was a forlorn hope, Hagar's mind was in the past. When Blair ran from the scene of his latest. crime he mounted a horse and at once rode to the railway station and boarded a train for Richmond. He 'could not, he told himself, live without Vivian, and at Mrs. Burton Randolph's he found her. He knew the risk he ran, because it was evident that some- one in Richmond had secured from Abe Bloom the dud cheque with the thumb print. But such was his fierce passion for the beautiful girl that he risked all to be With her once again. For herself, Vivian Marston had lingered in Richmond. leading an existence that was puritanical and galling to her in the vapid restraints, as she deemed them, of conventional southern social restrictiohs. Her one strong, eager, compelling desire, a desire that held her in provincial Richmond, was her desire for The Diamond from the Sky. ()nee, in all its blazing magnificence it had shone upon her breast. Her thoughts were upon it byi day, and at night her dreams were bright like gold and red like blood, a barbaric background for the dream diamond of these vivid visions.

Vivian sank her head upon Blair's breast beneath the wistaria bough., She tempted him with langurous glance and 'alluring lips. "Get and give me the diamond and I will be yours !" she whis- pered. And Blair, much as he had longed for the baleful jewel himself, now longed more for this woman.

"I shall never rest till I get it for you!" he replied fiercely. He would have kissed her to seal the contract, but she drew herself from his arms, and he knew that her caresses were only to be purchased by the diamond, ,be the price of the diamond lives, souls, or honour!

Vivian was an intensely practical young person, and going to the Bloom brothers she told them the history of the diamond, and they engaggcl the services of Tom Blake, the detective, to find the gem.

Where was the diamond? Strap McGee's walk through the farmyard led him to watch the negro boy. Advancing cautiously, he stood amazed at the sight of a wonderful jewel being used to tease a pig. Seizing a stick he belaboured the surprised boy, who bolted,. and then pelted the newcomer with anything he found handy. McGee, reaching' into the pig's trough, fished out the diamond and gleefully tripped away. Rich, for once very rich, Strap McGee walked into Richmond and sought sweet oblivion in Hung Li's opium den. In his new-found opulence he exhibited the diamond to an attendant and fell sound asleep. Hung Li, wishing to propitiate the great god Lung, robbed the drowsy tramp, and when Strap regained consciousness he missed the diamond.. He also missed the step, because the next moment the floor opened beneath him, and he was swallowed up. Again possession of the dia- mond had led to a sudden death.

That night, with Tom Blake as gnide, Mrs. Bur- ton Randolph decided to visit Chinatown, taking Blair and Vivian with her. 4t Hung Li's they met with opposition, and no wonder. The wily proprietor had hung the diamond round the neck of his god and was kneeling devoutly before the shrine when the visitors were announced. Blake would not be denied. His friends had come, he said, to burn incense for good luck before the great god . Lung. Pushing their way into the sanctuary they stood amazed. Gleaming on the breast of the vgly.image was The Diamond from the Sky! Hung Li's 'Seen ears noted the hisses of surp-'se, and the visitors were „hustled out. The ladies were sent home and the men separated, Blake to call up Abe Bloom and his brother and Blair to carry' out the whispered scheme of daring, Vivian. desperate man was soon cliMbing the fire- escape' at the back of the Chinese den. He had noted a' curtain-draped window behind tile joss. A crash of glass was heard within. The frenzied Hung Li rushed into the sanctuary, followed by the other Chinese, the two Blooms, the detective, and the bewildered Marmaduke, lac, had Come in search of Blair.

A hand through the broken glass behind the idol had torn The Diamond from the Sky from the neck anti 3.7;t.!f, el tin peat god Lung. With a demoniac scream " the lrentled Hung Li seixed a keen sword at the foot of the idol and aimed a swift blow at the disappearing hand, Woe and bad luck for ever tor ihe Unfortunate Hung Li. The blow had missed the despoiling white hand and severed the head of the great god. Another slash in excited frenzy, and down came the burning altar light, and the flimsy furnishings of the sanctuary and the whole den itself was a roaring blaze of fire. (To be continued.)

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT BOWLING ASSOCIATION.

War Fund Handicap. The Warrington and District Bowling Association have arranged what promises to be a highly suc- cessful war fund bowling handicap. Subscriptions are coming in quite encouragingly, and, so far, there are no fewer than 431 entries. The first rounds will be played to-day at the King and Queen grtund, Padgate-lane, when about half the players entered will be engaged, 240 figuring in 120 round.

TENNIS.

THE DELIGHTS OF BMX SEY PARK.

The Bewsey Park Tennis Club has had its vicis- situdes, but is now in a prosperous position. Several years ago its two courts were run privately, and then the Liberal Club rented them, but support being somewhat lacking, and marry people who wished to join being disqualified by their political trend, it was taken over by other ladies and gentle- en as well, and re-christened Bewsey Park Tennis Club,, 'under the chairmanship; of Mr. George Shawcross Pendlebury. . In a literani „moment we once described the courts and their surroundings as being the "post pleasant accretion of nature on the Bewsey side of the borough." Bewsey Park is famous for more than tennis, naturalists 'finding in it inexhaustible treasures, both of ;plant and bird life, and fre- quently within easy view of the tennis players, artists delight to pencil or paint•the beauties to be found there. _ •

The club itself is in a thriving condition. tat has over forty members at the present time, despite the fact that over twelve of its most regular players are in the firing line. The comforts and shades of the scene are augmented by ' two well-furnished pavilions, in which tea is usually served on Thurs- days and, Saturdays, for the social element of tennis is not at a discount, nor are competitive games, though outside of their own ranks the mem- bers have not gone since the war began. Frequently, however, tournaments take place,'all the members competing* for the . prizes given ,by the more generous-of themselves. One of those tournaments took Place last Saturday. Much of the success of the club is due to the energetic_ secretary, Mr. Chas:' Sinclair. The treasurer, Mr. Osborne Sinclair, is at the Front.

Here everything in the way of enjoyment associ- ated with tennis is at hand—keen players,' genial company, and ideal surroundings. Bewsey tennis players cast no envious glances towards Arcadian Cheshire!

SPORTS DAY AT PADGATE COTTAGE HOMES.

The children of the Padgate Cottage Homes and Schools had a very happy day on, Wednesday last The Guardians and officers had combined in arranging, a sports day for their benefit. Fortunately the day was very fine, and the chil- dren were able to enjoy themselves without re- straint in the large field attached to the sthool. Amongst the visitors present were: 'Miss C. Broadbent, Mrs. A. Hall, Rev. A. M. Mitchell, Mr. W. C. Brooks, Mr. W. J. Shaw, Mr. W. Flanagan, Mr. T. Dale, and Mr. T. Siddeley (Guardians), Dr. W. J. Peacocke (medical officer), Mr. and Mrs. Loxham (master and matron of the Workhouse), Mr. R. I. Partridge (assistant clerk to the Guardians) and Mrs. Partridge, Mrs. L. Jagger k superintendent nurse at the Union Infirmary), Mr: Hilditch, Mrs. and Miss Lunt, and a number of wounded soldiers from the Whitecross Hospital and Raddon Court. Tea was served to. the children al fresco, extras in the way of cherfitit strawberries and bananas being provided.

The Matron- entertained the visitors to tea on the lawn. At the conclusion, of the races the Chairman of the Cottage Homes Committee (Miss Broadbent) distributed the prizes, "which had been provided by subscriptions from the Guardians and officers, to the winners of the various events, and in doing so spoke of the excellent arrangements which had been made by the Superintendent and Matron (Mrs. A. E. Lowrance) and the staff. A most enjoyable and long-to-be-remembered day had been spent by all the children. Upon the motion of Mr. W. C. Brooks, Miss Broadbent was thanked for distributing the prize's, and cheers were given by the children for the Guardians, Matron and officers. Results :--

MEN WHO HAVE DONE THEIR PART.

The Warrington Tribunal sat on Thur,day, in the Town Hall. The Tribunal. divided and dis- posed of the eases in different rooms. Mr. E. Allen's Assistant Exempted.

Mr. E. Allen, wallpaper and paint Merchant, Buttermarket-street, applied for exempticin l'n behalf of an employe aged 38 years. Mr. Allen said this man was the only one he had left in the place. He formerly had his eldest boy with him, but he had joined the Army. In addition to the employe mentioned, he had only his daughter and a boy. His trade was rather a heavy one, and it required someone with strength to lift the the packages about. By Captain Mathams : This man was medically examined at the Orford Barracks about a couple of months • agti. The business was mainly con- cerned with domestic requirements. Mr. Allen further said that his eldestlioy was in the .41.rmy and his second son had been in the Navy, and had brought distinction 'upon Warrington by getting the Distinguished Ser- vice Cross in Gallipoli. He only mentioned this to show that he had done his part. The employe for whom he was appealing was a member of the Town Civic Force, and did not want to escape some amount' of liability in the public service. Canditional'exemption was granted. ' Solicitor's Application.

Mr. Arthur Browne, solicitor, applied for exemption for a managing clerk, aged 34 years,. married, who has sole management of all...com- mon law actions and cases at Assizes, Sessions, County Courts and Police Courts. He had been with him for nearly twenty-one years. He had now 'only four out of an original staff of nine. Recently. a lady typist had left him for a posi- tion in the National Insurance Offices, and another clerk, aged 18, joincd the Navy. His own 'fon, an drticled apprentice, had joined the Rifle Brigade. _

The Criairman (Dr. Joseph, Deputy Mayor) said he Tribunal had some parallel cases to consider—. They would deal with each case according to its, merits, and announce their decision later. I Friendly Society Treasurer.

An appeal was entered by the Trustees of the "Court Mercy" of the Ancient Order of Foresters, asking that their treasurer, aged 36 years, should be exempted. Mr. T. S. Steel, who appeared in support of the application, said that this man was one of four employes df Messrs. George Allen and Sons, who had re- ceived badges, but in case he might lose his employment and his badge was taken away it was desired that the Tribunal should decide this application. The man, as treasurer, had to attend the registered offices three times each week to receive the subscriptions ot members, and he had also to keep the books of the society The membership of the society is 260 in the voluntary and 320 in the State section. There was a scheme for reorganisation under the 'National Insurance Act about being formulated. In reply to the Chairman it was stated that only a member of the society could be elected to the position. The majority did not take an active interest in the Society.

The Chairman: There should be an under- study.

Mr. Steel: We can't convince them op. that. The Chairman: Well, We had better see what we can do. (Laughter.)

The appeal was refused, Dr. Joseph remarking that the question of the man being badged was another matter.

Butcher With Large Contracts. Mr. C. H. Unsworth, solicitor, appealed on behalf of a well-known butcher, and explained that the applicant, who was the sole proprietor of a large business in the town, had contracts pending with the Warrington Union, White- cross Hospital, Warrington Infirmary,' and many other larc'e .contracts of a public nature. .11e submitted that without taking up their valu- able time this was a case that merited their favourable consideration)

WORKING MEN'S MISSION AND THE CONSCIENTIOUS OBJECTOR.

In our report of the proceedings of the War- rington- Military Tribunal, a few weeks ago, when a- claim for exemption, on conscientious •grounds, was made by Mr. Alfred Walker, 98, Liverpool-road, we stated that letters were read from the Working Men's Mission "in support of Mr. Walker's statements.", This summary of what happened, though quite cor- rect, has been misconstrued. The Mission offi- cials who wrote the letter—quite unsolicited by the applicant—by instruction of their com- mittee, did so to testify, as they were in duty bound to do, to the bona-fides of Mr. Walker's conscientious scruples. He was a member of the Mission and they had known him to t-nter- lain pacifist views for. years, and said SO. That did not mean, as it has been taken to mean, that the Mission officials associated themselves with their ,friend's pacifist views; to say that they knew .he 'entertained those views was not to say they themselves entertained them. The Mission's War Record.

The Mission's war record should be suffi- ciently conFincing as to the views of the great majority of the .worleers. Well over 1,500 woollen comforts-have been sent to the men in the Forces. Early this year £3OO was in- vested by the Mission is 5 percent. war bonds. A 11ranch of the War Savings Association (with about 100 members) was formed at the Mission about a‘ month ago, and already over £l2O has been subscribed from this source. The Mission roll of honour contains the names of 42 mem- bers, most of whom volunteered in the first few months_ of the war; and the roll could have been increased by scores of names if the rule laid down not to include anyone who was not a•bona-fide member at the time of enlisting had not been strictly. observed.

ANONYMOUS LETTERS.

Medical,

PREPARED TO LEAVE THE COUNTRY.

Mr. Charles Dukes appeared before the Man- Ichester Tribunal on Thursday, in the role of a conscientious objector and, after an interesting sitting in which there were some piquant pas- sages, had his application for exemption dis- Missed because. as the /Chairman (Mr. Good- behere) said, "he has established no 'objection on conscientious grounds." When Mr., Dukes took his seat well within arm's length of all the members of the Tribunal and the military representative—for_ it was a small Court, there being five sections of the Tribunal working simultaneously, • the room being petitioned into cabinets—Colonel McClure explained that he was 35 years of age, a trade union organiser, and declared, "As a Socialist I have a conscientious objection to all wars, in- cluding the present." _ _ _

Mr. Dukes now augmented this by stating that he had been a Socialist for upwards of ten years. during which time. he had been an active public propagandist, and had always hail anti-militarist convictions. He had a moral objection to taking part in the military prosecution of war. Long prior to the war fie took part in anti-militarist agitation, and the armaments agitation; also in debating against various , forms of compulsory military service. Because of those convictions he had a con- scientious objection to acting in any war. Colonel McClure: You have already appealed before the Tribunal on ccinscientious grounds? Applicant: No. My employers appealed and I was prevented from stating my conscientious objections by the Chairman of that Tribunal (Mr. Brierley) saying "This is an employer's appeal." Colonel McClure: And it was dismissed. But I think, with all due. respect, that your 'eon. victions were, stated then, and Mr. Brierley declined to be -.

Applicant: Mr. Brierley said, We cannot review your own 'case' as this is an employers' appeal!' . I have since been informed that my personal appeal which was then submitted had been lost and, as I hold a receipt for it, Colonel Wynter allowed me to ledge another. And that I ana now here to present to, you. Colonel McClure: Your grounds are really more political than religious? • Applicant: Well, ;do not think so. I have a moral objection tO

What is- your moial objection?—l object to taking an part in what I term international confliet. which is not in accordance 'with my Socialistic 'convictions, and is of no advantage from my concept of moral advancement. A Member of the Tribunal: supposing you met a man VI the street and he went for you; what would you do? Would you defend your- self ?—Yes, of course I would in a civil capacity. That is totally different ,from my convictions regarding the use of -physical force in a mili- tary sense.

You .do believe in physical force?-4 do not. • But you would resist physical fOrce?--Well, yes, certainly if it is imposed upon me.

APPLICANT'S ANTI-MILITARIST RECORD. Colonel McClure: You have been somewhat bragging that "the Army won't drag me in"?— I am going to deny.., that. Have you got any evidence in support of that? If you haven't ask, the Chairman not to take the suggestion into account. Is it true, though P—lt is not true. I deny it emphatically. Colonel George Crosfield, Captain Townroe and every one in Warrington knows that I am sincere in my beliefs. But what have you been saying?—lf I had to reiterate everything you would be here a month. Give us some of it ?—I have said that I will take no part in the prosecution of this war. That, I think, summarises everything. I notice, Colonel, that you have some Press cut- tings in your hand. May I hand in one from the "Warrington Examiner," which should prove the sincerity of my convictions? Mr. Dukes then gave the chairm4n a quota- tion from a leading articles in our columns, and that gentleman read as follows It would be interesting to know for whom the Warrington Trades and Labour Council speak nowadays. We notice that when a deputation wat, sent to represent this body :it the congress of delegates of Trade Unions last week, a claim was made on behalf, of Mr, ' Chas. Dukes—thdugh we do not know ,that it was with his samtion—that he represented 10,000 Warrington workers. Does he? Mr. Chas. Dukes has been persistently in opposi- tion to all movements that ,have been under- taken for the better and successful prosecu- tion ol the war. He 'was opposed to mili- tarism even in the mild form in which we understood it before the war. The Chairman having read it aloud, looked over his eyeglasses at Mr. Dukes as if waiting for his comment,' whereupon the applicant remarked, "The statement that I was opposed to militarism before the war is true, 'and I take it that that quotation is a testimony to my con- victions." The Chairman: As a trade union organiser have you ever taken part in the use of physical force to enforce your requirements?—No, in ns) form of physical force, and when there was a movement on foot in 1911 for the arming nf trade unionists I opposed it. ,

STRIKES AND VIOLENCE.

The Chairman: Have. you .brought your men. out on strike?—As an agent or a trade union I have been invested with the conduct of strikes. In a diversion Mr:. Dukes mentioned that when the war broke out he placed his resigna- tion in the hands of his union, stating that he had no intention of taking part in the-war, and the union could accept it if they disagreed with him. They had, however, allowed him freedom of conscience. The Chairman: When the union had a strike on did you resign then, because you were opposed to physical force P—No. A strike is a cessation of a contract, and does not imply the use of physical force.

Colonel McClure: You know what the natural consequences are?—We do all we can to dis- courage violence; but, as a matter of fact, I have known rows to be started by the police themselves. . Later. Mr. Dukes said he would like to ask Colonel McClure a question if permission could be given. Colonel McClure: You can, but I do not say I shall answer it.

STOCKTON LODGE SCHOOL.

Although, there was thunder. and heavy rain at Stockton Heath yesterday, a goodly number of parents and friends assembled in the Church Room, when successful pupils Of the Stockton Lodge School for girls and ,kindergarten and preparatory' for boys, . were presented with cer- tificates and prizes. Rev. L. J. T. Darwall, vicar of Walton, pre- sided, and distributed the prizes. Miss E. A. Partington, principal, said that since two years ago several new features , had been introduced, including the Oxford Local Examination, preliminary junior and senior. Ari entirely new feature in the school was the commercial course, shorthand and typevriting. which she 'hoped would be taken up more and more. They had a new 'mistress in elocution— Miss Forsyth—and she (Miss Partington) , pro- 'posed to . enter students in the .examination arranged ''ay the British Instity.tion.. Certificates and prizes having been presented, Rev. L. J. T. Darwall delivered an interesting address.

COMMERCIAL TRAINING.

Licensee On The Jealousy Of Alderman Bennett Enthusiastic Over Neighbours. Secondary School Innovation.

Remarkable statements were made at the War- rington Rural District Council on Wednesday afternoon, when a well-known Sankey gridges licensee, who had been exempted for two months, appealed for an extension. He is thirty-two years old, married, and his wife is at present in a delicate state of health. _ _

Mr. Arthur BrOwne, solicitor, said that if his client had to enter the Army his business would have to be given ,up owing to the state of his wife's health, as it was impossible for her to attend to it. Since the adjournment the appli- cant had thought it his national duty to do something more, and he was now engaged on a farm at Sankey, working firom seven o'clock in the morning until six o'clock in the evening. The house was a fairly busy one, and at week- ends six people were required to serve. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: Isn't that the very thing the Governmett are complaining about— too much drinking? Mr. IV. Furness (the military representative): You said at a previous inquiry that it was necessary to have a man in the house owing to the number of Irishmen who called there? , Applicant: YE S. Mr. Furness: I am told that they resented that and now taboo the,house, so a man is not required to be there.

Applicant: They have not resented anything I said, for they come as usual. I hope you are not taking any notice of anonymous letters, for it is nothing but jealousy on the part of people who wanted to get the house when I did. They have been jealous.ever since.

Mr. Furness said that there were many cases where the owners of licensed houses had trans- ferred the licence to the wives of the license. Applica,nt: Take into account ray wife's deli- cate state of health. On the appeal bemg dismissed,' Mr: Furne*Si said to the applicant: If there is any delay in the transfer and you want a little time, I think we can arrange with Mr. Browne. Colonel Ireland Blackburne's Timber. In appealing for an employe,. There the finest ash in the country was to be found, and it was going to be used forthe Ggvernznent. He considered the Colonel had o atted very patriotically in allowing it the Allied. Timber was very much needed at the pi•eQent time, and he believed that from 15,000 to 20,000 Canadians had been brought from Canada in order to cut down frees. In reply to the Chairman (Mr. George Mather) Mr. Smith acknowledged that'the man was not an expert at felling timber, but added that "he is most adaptable." Temporary exemption until September 30th. Withdrawn Badges. In the course of an application made on be- half of several men employed at Penketh Tannery, Mr. Murphy reparked that the firm had agreed to .withdraw the badges from five single men. The Chairman: It is far better for the single than the married men to go. Mr. Murphy: Through unbadging these men it means, of course, a lessened output.

j'he Education Committee have approved suggestions for the introduction of commercial training in the Secondary School. REGISTER OF BRITISH MANUFACTURERS.

Chamber Of Commerce And Trade ' After The War.

The manufacturers of Warrington have evi- dently realised the importance of the nroiect initiated by the Warrington Chamber of Com- merce in association with some thirty-five lead- ing Chambers of the United Kingdom. Busi-1 ness men generally appreciate the necessjty of promoting • " Trakle after the War" and the urgency ,of making preparation to absorb. the , enemy's commerce. The Proposed publication qf a Year Book in five languages is a step in the right direction, and, as the wdrk has the i official endorsement of such a large number of leading Chambers, it will undoubtedly carry great weight with buyers abroad. It will be seen by the list published in this issue that the. leading, Manufacturers of War- rington , have now , definitely decided to co- operate with 'the Chamber in making the book ,a success. The circulation will be 'unique, as• the hook will he distributed Post free to the Diplomatic Corps, Chambers of Commerce, Boards of Trade, and a selected list of buyers throughout the world. Theie is no doubt that a publication of this nature is badly needed by over-sea buyers, and it is hoped that a// manufacturers in Warring- ton and the district will loyally support the Chamber in its effort to advance' the commercial and manufacturing interests of the town. The Chamber's special representative, Mr. A. Sydney Watson, M.A., will be pleased to call on any manufacturer who may desire further details by appointment through the Secretary, Alder. man Bennett. Death of a Former Lord Derby War Hospital Patient. Trooper W. E. Thomas, clif the New Zealand Contingent, who was wounded at the Dardanelles, and was an inmate- of the Lord Deihy Hospital for many months, has died suddenly in New Zealand. Trooper Thomas made the acquaintance of quite a number of friends while in Warrington, all of whom will be sorry to learn of his death.

NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN that owing to the increase in the price of Coal and the greatly increased expenses due to the War, the Price of Electric Clirrent •as and from the Ist August, 1916. will be increased as follows:— For Lighting purposes and for Traniways 5 per cent. in addition to the existing scale of charges. For Heating and Power purposes (except in cases provided for in which the charges for current are regulated by the price of coal), 5 per cent, in addition to the existing scale of charges. Dated this 21st day of July. 1916. J. LYON WHITTLE, Town Clerk.

Warrington, Runcorn & District Savings Bank. Established 1818. HEAD OFFICE - BUTTERMARKET STIJEET. ( BANK QUAY, 29, GREEN STREET. BR I\CIIES - - EARLESTOAN N, 5, QUEEN STREET. RUNCORN OFFICE - 111611 STREET.

majority still cling slavishly to the old one-pump- ful-every-ten-miles theory, unless, of course, the machine is automatically lubricated. Only those who know that the pressure on the connecting rod . is somewhere round 3,0001 b. to the square inch; who know the liability of bearings subjected to this immense strain to split; who know how quickly the heat of friction will destroy the temper of the finest metal, can realise the important part good oil plays on a motor engine, and the necessity for maintain- ing a constant film of oil over all wearing surfaces. Good oil, being composed of tiny globulei, acts in a very similar way to the balls in a bal bearing and , reduces wear and tear, friction and heat to a mini- mum. OILING A MOTOR CAR ENGINE. Grease All caps on the steering gear and its con- nections once a week, and oil the clutch fork, throttle and ignition controls, gear changing con- nections, brake joints and torque-rod joints also once a week. Grease the spring shackle and pin cups, the fan bearing and the clutch spigot bearing every six hundred miles or so, and the front hubs, the leaves of the springs, the steering gear box, back [ axle, gear box, universal joints, speedometer bear- ings every thousand miles or so. Oil the magneto bearings every thousand miles or so. Clean 'the Plugs once a month, test the brakes before every run, and always start out with clean lamps. LOOK TO YOUR RADIATOR. Overheating the water in the radiator is much more prevalent during hot than during cold weather. This is only natural, of course. But it is a trouble that is continually cropping up throughout the year. There are many causes of this overheating, but nearly always it is lack of attention on the part of the driver. It is only the work of a minute to test the quantity of water in the system, and to add the necessary amount to bring it up to the correct level. It is not sufficient only to fill up at the beginning of a very long journey, since if hilly roads be encountered the water will evaporate rapidly, and thit without any discernible steam being given off. When a very long hill has to be negotiated it is advisable to see to the radiator at the bottom, and not to wait until part way up before testing. There are some cars, however, which re- quire little attention, but they are among the minority. With the thermo-syphon system he water does not circulate so freely if the radiator be filled to the top. Just fill it to the correct level to produce the best results. You must determine for yourself what level this is. , SPOKES. LONG SERVICE. Presentation To Mr. Edward Plinston By Assurance Agents. In recognition of a quarter of a cenbiry's zealous services to the Warrington Branch of the National Union of Life Assurance Agents, Mr. Edward Plinston was presented on Friday of Inst week with a large portrait of 'himself and an expensive Swan fountain pen. The photograph, framed in 'fumed oak, is a capital likeness of the branch president. The presentation was made in the Freedom Fellow- ship Room, Market Gate, and Mr. George Six- smith presided. • Mr. Edwin Plinstoni in handing over the gifts, said the portrait would be long appreci- ated, representing as it did one of Warrington's most worthy sons. Mr. B. Warburton said their president had not only been a great help to that branch of the National Union, but also to the Royal Liver Union, of which he was a member. , Mr. Joseph Whitehouse said their association with and friendship for Mr. Edward Plinston were always of the best. He always had the interests of the union at heart. Mr. James Hey-wood testified to the hard work done by the old members and especially by Mr. Plinston, with whom he had been happily associated for forty years. ' Kindly references were also made by other gentlemen, and Mr. Plinston, in thanking the members for their beautiful present, said he ' would long remember, if his life was spared, their kind expressions of brotherhood. In the course of some interesting reminiscences, he said he began his insurance career on July 17th, 1874, forty-two years ago, with the Refuge Friendly Society. The National Union was in- augurated August 27th, 1884, and he was pre- sent at the opening of their local branch. It was a revelation to him the number of cases their union had had to deal with. Lord Lans- downe's Bill was a blow to insurance workers; the State Insurance 'was worse, and now the block system, their mightiest foe, was at their doors. A plate affixed to the portrait frame bears the inscription "Presented to Edward Plinston, Esq., President Of the Warrington Branch of the National Union of Life Assurance Agents, in recognition of services rendered during twenty-five years' membership." Refreshments were served during the interval, followed by a pleasant whist drive. THE WAR BUDGET. An outstanding feature in the current issue of the "War Budget" is a series of official photographs taken on the field during the British offensive. These pictures will never lose their accurate historical value. In these expensive times the issue is an obviously, generous threepennyworth. Many an orator permits his lungs to run away . with his logic.

Co., has been. wounded. Sergeant Newbold joined the King's Royal Rifles a month after' war was declared, went to France in July- last year, and came home ,on a short furlough on May 10th. During a night attack last Monday week a shell fr#gment struck his right hand, and he is now ut a base hospital in France. in a letter to, his father he says he is ,progressing favourably. His brother, Corporal Newbold, who joined the Army ,at the same time, was wounded a few months ago. Sergeant Newbold, a Secondary School old boy, was on the goods manager's staff of the L. and N.-W. Railway at Warrington. He is a well-known playing member of the Latchford Hockey Club and the Penketh Tennis Club. He has had some exciting experiences in France, and' has recently been largely engaged as a grenade instructor. PRIX-ATE J. • CURLEY. Comrade Writes That He Is Wounded .In The Arm. Private John Curley, of the Lancashire Fusiliers, a married man, 55, Watkin-street, has, according to a letter received by his wife from a comrade, been wounded in the arm. Private Curley, however, has not yet written himself. Private Curley, who is 29 years of age, has been in the Army since the 'first month of the war and has served in France for 'the, past twelve months. He was employed before en- listing by Messrs. Williams, sculptors. He was educated at St. Mary's R.C. School, Buttenmarket-syeet.

Rifleman Joseph Shaw, Rifle Brigade, 11, Gandy's Walk, Tilley-street, son of the late Mr. Samuel Shaw, butcher; has been severely wounded, a German bullet having passed through his right shoulder, entering ,at the front and emerging from the back, having in transit shattered the bones of the shoulder at the joint. lie and a comrade were hit during the hot fighting on July 10th, and they sought shelter in a hole; one being hurt in the• arm and the other in the leg, they were able to assist eas:h• other somewhat. They remained in the hole 26 hours, and then crawled to a clug-out where they I were, happily, found by the R:A.M.C. They had both lost a great deal of blood, but are now in hopes of recovering. Rifleman Shaw is in a Stockport Military Hospital, where his wife saw him last Wednes- day. He was in the best of spirits and wits quite hopeful, from what he had seen, •that the

Having received iaews from her husband, Private William Wareing, of the King's Royal Rifles, that he was, wounded, Mrs. Wareing, 26, Cobden-street, this week paid a visit to a Lon- don hospital, and there found him suffering from shrapnel wounds in the shin-bone, sus- tained during the first five minutes of an attack on the enemy's trenches. Rifleman Wareing, who is 38 years of age, was formerly employed in the Dalton Bank wiredrawing departnient of Messrs. Rylands Bros.' works. He enlisted in April, 1915, and has served at the Front for nine months. As a boy he attended the Arpley-street and Wycliffe Schools. PRIVATE F. S. BOWDEN. Warrington, have received a letter from their son, Signaller Harry Walms- , ley, of the Royal Field Artillery, stating that he is wounded- and nov lips in Epsom Hospital, County of London. "I am very glad to tell you it is only a slight wound," he writes. "(I suppose I will be as right as ever again in aboitt a month's time. To tell you the truth, I was very glad when received .my wound; for. it has been hell upon • earth" out there for the last fort- night. I any. one in a thousand._ to gett a nice wound. I never saw a more cowardly lot than the Germans; they can't face our men. They throw up their hands above their heads, ,shout- ing' for mercy, when our chaps get near them. PUBLIC AND AIR-RAID RELICS.' . With reference to the Order in Council of the 10th May, amending' the Defence of the Realm Regulations, the War Office request that the public, will render assistance by notifying at once to the military' authorities, or to a police constable in the neighbourhood, the find- ing of any bomb or projectile or fragment thereof, or any other article discharged, dropped, or lost from any enemy aircraft or veesel.

RIFLEMAN JOS. TAYLOR. Was Expecting His Good Luck To See Him Through. A letter written by an Army chaplain has conveyed to Mr. and Mrs. Taylor, 218, Knuts- ford-road, the news of the death in action of their son, Rifleman Joseph Taylor, of the Rifle Brigade. The letter ran as follows :—"I am very sorry to inform you that your son has been killed in action in the recent battle. I hope you will try to bear the loss bravely and

pride the appreciation of his self-sacrifice." News has not yet been received from the War Office. Rifleman Taylor himself last wrote home on the 27th of last month. "We have just come out of the trenches," he said, "and we go in again in another day or so. We are- going to have another, 'go ' at driving them back, so you Might be reading some good. news • -.core this letter reaches you. Ot course some of us will be missing when the roll is called, but I tm expecting my good luck to pull me through again." Rifleman Taylor, who was an only son, and 28 years of age, was formerly employed as a moulder at the Richmond Gas Stove and Meter Co., Ltd. He enlisted on the 3rd September, 1914, and landed in France on March 17th, 1915. He was educated at the St. Mary's R.C.. School, ButtermarketAreet. PRIVATE S. S. SHAW. While being conveyed from the battle area to a hospital, Rifleman John Taylor, of 9, Ernest- street, succumbed to .his wounds, in a French ambulance a few days ago. This brave and efficient soldier was wounded iii May, 1915, and left for the Front again on Christmas Eve. He was an athlete of quite conspicuous merit, and played regularly for certain football teams, in- cluding that of Pochin's. A letter sent by the gallant soldier to his mother, Mrs. ,Gresty, Orchard- road, Sandy-lane, states that he is at present in the Bristol Military Hospital, wounded in the knee and "doing fine." He also pays a great tribute to the treatment he receives from the officials at the hospital. Private Jeck Gresty is a well-known local man, and is exceedingly popular in football circles, being a prominent member of the Oughtrington Amateur A.F.C. He is 25 years of age, and was educated at Olightrington School and worshipped at Oughtrington Parish Church. PADGATE. Picnic.—The members of the Padgate Wes- leyan Choir had a very enjoyable outing 'en Saturday afternoon to Liverpool and New Brighton. After tea a visit was paid to Wallasey village, the return journey being made along Harrison-drive on to the sands and past the Red Noses to New Brightoif, returning from the latter to Liverpool, and thence by the 9.30 to Warrington. The weather, which had been very unsettled for some days, turned out beautifully fine, which made the outing very beneficial and enjoyable.
